#7260. Campaign Trail: Tiffany tells human holiday story with Hollywood talent

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The luxury brand and agency American Haiku leaned into real emotions with actor Amanda Seyfried and director Derek Cianfrance.

In adland, it has been a decidedly handmade holiday season, with brands including Levi’s and E.l.f. turning to techniques like stop-motion animation to connect with consumers who are growing weary of ads created by artificial intelligence.

For its annual holiday effort, Tiffany & Co. found the best way to resonate with consumers is through human storytelling. The luxury brand’s global campaign, “Love is a Gift,” was created with agency American Haiku and revolves around a two-minute film starring actor Amanda Seyfried and director Derek Cianfrance.

The short, “You & Me,” jumps from the present day to New York City in the 1990s to tell the story of a young girl and her mother, played by Seyfried. The girl watches her mother work as a choreographer and has a handful of holiday experiences before being gifted with a Tiffany ornament that she shares with her own daughter in the present.

“Moving between past and present, the film reflects the enduring place Tiffany & Co. holds in life’s most meaningful moments and the stories we carry forward,” said CMO Andrea Davey in a statement.

The film is soundtracked by a cover of The Wannadies’ “You & Me Song,” a tune best known for its appearance on the 1996 “Romeo + Juliet” soundtrack. The campaign will run across cinema, paid and organic social, digital out-of-home, owned channels and clienteling across global channels.

Along with being on-trend with regard to stories imbued with humanity, Tiffany’s holiday campaign also represents a pivot from the brand’s past holiday creative. The 2025 edition of the “Love is a Gift” campaign turned its iconic blue boxes into portals to love stories around the world. This year, Tiffany brought on American Haiku, which had worked on a previous campaign for the brand in the U.K., but didn’t offer a traditional brief.

“They came to us with an initial idea of a family spending the holidays in their home and [tapping] into the memory aspect,” said Ben Muckensturm, creative director at the shop. “Where we pushed the work was bringing in the importance of the mother-daughter relationship that I think is the core of the story.”

Keying into the mother-daughter story gave the brand and agency a larger insight around the memory of seeing a parent as a person in their own world that is relatable to consumers, whether or not they are wealthy residents of the Upper East Side like the characters in the film. In the film, the true gift is not the ornament but the memory that is associated with it.

“That opened us up to how we explore the past and then bring it back to the present and create a piece that feels essentially real, that anyone could connect with,” Muckensturm said. “Everything was shot in New York. It was definitely an intentional push to try to bring it to the real world and make it more of an emotional piece.”

“You & Me” also delivers nostalgia for a pre-digital ‘90s that has been a reliable way to engage millennial audiences. However, the nostalgia in the film is not the traditional touchstones, like technology and boy bands, used by marketers looking to tap into memories of the decade. Instead, Tiffany looked at its own archives and at the holidays of movies like “Home Alone” and “The Parent Trap” for inspiration.

“When we created the town house, we went back to old archival Tiffany imagery of how things were,” Muckensturm said of creating the main set for the ad. “Especially on Fifth Avenue, most of the references that we brought were from ‘Home Alone 2,’ like what the streets looked like at the time. It's great to be able to tap into things that people are familiar with.”

After creating the world of the ad, the agency populated it with brand ambassador Seyfried, whose character is the mother in the past, rather than the main character in the present-day framing device. The actor, an Academy Award nominee whose performance in one of the year’s biggest films has captivated audiences, brought her gifts to the production.

“She has such a maternal instinct that really played perfectly on set, and you could see really how she was a mother to that girl for the days that we shot,” Muckensturm said.

The choice of Cianfrance, a director best known for dramatic fare like “Blue Valentine” and “The Place Beyond the Pines,” helped the brand and agency get the emotional tone right, without rehearsal and without falling into familiar or even corny holiday tropes.

“There was a trusting relationship between production partners, us and the client to come into it with a really open mind to see what the scene gives us,” Muckensturm said. “We knew where we were going to shoot, and we were trying to get a range of emotion… but we really let him direct them and let this play naturally.”

Ten-figure acquisitions, new agency experiments and organizational changes are underway, but the real key may be relinquishing a sense of control.

#7258. Anthropic says no to ads but Wall Street may have other ideas

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Anthropic is a public company now, which puts it on an unofficial path to an ads business.

Unofficial because the company’s official position is still firmly against ads. It went as far as spending Super Bowl money on a campaign mocking OpenAI for bringing them to ChatGPT. Its prospectus, the document pitching those public shares to investors, leaves advertisers out of the business altogether.

“Is it cynical to expect Anthropic to follow the same route? Not really. More like an educated guess,” said ad tech expert Shirley Marschall.

Besides, the company has already left the door open, she continued. Its pledge rules out sponsored links adjacent to conversations, but in the same post it says it will keep building features that help users “find, compare, or buy products, connect with businesses.” And once an agent recommends products, retail media will pay to be in those recommendations, Marschall said, before adding: “Let’s see Anthropic turn that ad revenue down,” she added.

Advertisers, for their part, will be watching closely, and with mixed feelings. On the one hand, a new media seller means a new platform to wrangle and one more set of tools and teams to learn at a time when most are still working out how to buy ChatGPT. On the other hand, Claude’s audience is also spending more and more time in those conversation threads. For marketers, that’s a hard place to stay out of for long.

“Anthropic, like any company, has a stance towards handling consumer data and inadvertently how advertising is considered,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Souza Rauto. “But monetization is a lucrative form of revenue. I’d expect Anthropic to have a change of heart. Look no further to how Apple has played this advertising game over the past few years despite being considered anti-advertising.”

Life as a public company isn’t cheap, after all, and it’s even less cheap for an AI company that has signed up for approximately $895.4 billion in compute and infrastructure commitments, some running to 2045. That’s more than 13 years of revenue at its current pace, given Q3 revenue of $17 billion. The revenue covering that bill is far less locked in since 80% of revenue is consumption-based and the S-1 says many customers can cut usage or terminate on short notice. In fact, the company has already warned that “operating margins may compress when large tranches arrive.”

Then there’s the free tier. “Free and promotional users may never convert to paid plans,” the filing admits, yet Anthropic still pays to serve every one of them. Analysts found that cost sits in sales and marketing out of sight of the headline margin. That arrangement holds as long as free users keep upgrading. If they stop, Anthropic is left with a growing audience it pays to serve and earns nothing from.

OpenAI has already shown what that audience could be worth. ChatGPT ads reached a $1 billion annualized revenue run rate less than 200 days after launching. When both companies are trading, analysts will ask Anthropic why it’s leaving money on the table.

“They’ll go after advertising, just not in the way some assume,” said Rob Webster, founder and CEO of TAU Marketing Solutions. “The prize for Anthropic isn’t ads inside Claude. Advertising is now a trillion-dollar market, and the real power sits with whatever brain coordinates it: deciding what runs, where, and why. That’s a far more valuable position than selling impressions. Anthropic wants to be a new, better Microsoft or Apple. Selling tools to businesses. Not ad opportunities.”

There are reasons, though, why Anthropic may resist the lure of ads for as long as it feasibly can.

“Trust and impartiality really matter with LLMs, particularly among enterprise customers,” said Alex Tait, founder of Entropy. “In contrast to OpenAI, Anthropic is currently approaching operating break-even without ads suggesting its existing business model could be commercially sustainable without it.”

Anthropic’s anti-ads stance is based on its perspective that once ads become part of a business’s revenue targets, the pressure to maximize engagement can start influencing product decisions. For an AI app, in this case Claude, that creates a conflict: users expect Claude to help them make decisions in their own interests, not towards whatever generates revenue for the company or its advertisers. And in its IPO prospectus, the company said that they want “Claude’s only incentive to be giving them [users] a helpful answer,” which is why they have “chosen not to introduce that pressure [ads] into Claude.”

Anthropic did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment.

Walmart is allowing access to Meta’s Muse through a gateway that lets it control how AI agents browse, shop and make purchases.

Amazon is paying to reach ChatGPT shoppers while the chatbot is finding other ways to surface its products and deals.

#7256. Future of Marketing Briefing: Consulting firms revive their marketing ambitions in the C-suite the holdcos covet

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of Marketing Briefing covers the latest in marketing for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Friday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

However the holdcos settle the question of whether the CEO route counts as due diligence, Deloitte Digital and Accenture Song got there first, and both have started selling marketing in the C-suite with a fresh push.

Deloitte Digital’s offer is Converge, an operating system that strings together AI agents to run marketing and sales work, from research and briefs through to campaign deployment and deal tracking. Some of the agents are Deloitte’s own. Others are ones a client already runs elsewhere, and the whole thing plugs into the tools and clout contracts a brand is already paying for. Marketing and sales versions are live, with commerce on the way, and Deloitte pitches them as one front-office system tied to revenue, which it calls “brand to cash.” Because it’s sold as a license that stretches across sales, marketing and service, it needs sign-off well above the CMO, which suits a consulting firm that’s used to having those conversations.

Accenture Song’s play in media, a market it has circled in the U.S. for years without committing. In September, it moved Melissa Fein, who built its media planning and buying business in Australia and New Zealand after leaving Initiative in 2024, to New York as U.S. media lead. This month it posted a job for a head of media strategy for the Americas. The ad describes a “new world media practice” with no legacy systems to protect, and asks for an AI-first strategist” to lead planning, build new tools and become the person senior clients call before a brief exists.

Candidates need hands-on command of econometrics, incrementality testing and attribution plus experience in big-spending categories like financial services, telecommunications, automotive and health. Those are the categories where the ANZ team won Australia Post and Mediabank. Accenture Song Ndidi Oteh has kept quiet on specifics, saying only that whatever it does in media won’t take traditional forms.

“The real prize is the conversation before the brief,” said  Steve Boehler, founder of agency consultancy the Mercer Island Group. ”If you help define the business problem, design the operating model and decide how success will be measured, you are competing long before an agency review begins.”

Both plays echo what the holdcos have spent 18 months engineering: operating systems that run a client’s marketing for it and media businesses that sell more than buying. The consultants may have the better shot at making either one stick. They already sit with the CEOs, CIOs and CFOs who sign off on systems spanning sales, marketing and service and installing that kind of software is their day job. A holdco operating system, rightly or wrongly, still reads as an agency tool, built partly to make the agency cheaper to run. Accenture also arrives in the U.S. media with no legacy buying business to protect, so it can lead with measurement and tools before it has to sell buying power.

Whether any of this translates is another matter. Consultancies have been trying to crack advertising for the best part of a decade, after all, and the media buying business still rewards a scale Accenture doesn’t yet have in the U.S. Then again, now is arguably as good a time as any for them to try, if not better. With Omnicom and IPG combined, big advertisers are down to three holdcos, and some are skipping the pitch one so a credible alternative has rarely been more useful. AI is also pushing decisions about how marketing works into rooms where the CFO and CIO sit, which consultancies have been in for years. And a C-suite that takes a closer look at marketing will start asking how agencies make their money, from data fees to principal media, which is an awkward question for any firm with a buying business to defend.

“The consultancies may have an advantage in getting invited to design the system,” said Boehler. “That is not the same as proving they can run marketing better. The client still needs evidence of better decisions, better work and better business outcomes, not just fewer handoffs.”

All of which makes the Publicis-WPP episode look a little quaint, partly because it was never quite the fight it was billed as. Sadoun spent months telling investors that Publicis was winning big accounts without a pitch. Rose, asked about it by an analyst, called it “this pitchless CEO handshake thing that I keep getting asked about” and said decisions that big deserve careful consideration. Publicis answered with an open letter treating that line as her theory, and the coverage did the rest. Somewhere along the way, a question about how clients choose agencies turned into a row about manners.

What got lost was the shift both CEOs were describing. Big advertisers are increasingly buying data, identity and AI infrastructure, which a six-week parade of slide decks was never built to judge, so some now test one partner in depth instead of auditioning several. Those decisions are also moving up the organization chart, to executives who sign off on systems not campaigns. That’s how consultancies have always been bought. Deloitte and Accenture are built for long, technical evaluations run by CFOs and CIOs, and while the holdcos; remarks were being turned into a grudge march one launched a system that needs the c-suite’s signature and the other started building a U.S. media practice designed to win it.

“Client CEOs and CMOs pay attention to CEOs of holdcos, especially when a transition in agency leadership occurs and the agency CEO conducts outreach to key clients,” said Gartner analyst Jay Wilson. “But most recognize that while agency CEOs may set a direction for the agency, that direction may be slow to take hold – or never take hold – within the agency teams doing the actual day-to- day work.  A strong relationship with, or aversion to, an agency chief executive is usually more consequential at smaller, independent agencies.”

Whatever gets agreed in the c-suite, someone still has to do the work.

“The consultants don’t have to copy the incumbent model to compete,” said Boehler. “But they still have to do the work: plan, negotiate, deliver, reconcile and prove results. A different model is a proposition until clients experience the difference.”

Cinema admissions have climbed back on the strength of a fuller blockbuster slate and much of the ad money that shifted to digital during lockdowns stayed put. Most screens still sell their preshow through sales houses that have always sold offline.

Mikkel Hagedorn, a WPP and Omnicom alum, has spent five or six years building a platform that lets buyers book cinema spots programmatically, target them against specific films and count impressions straight off ticket sales. The company is live in Denmark and Finland, where sales houses have handed it two minutes of a 15-minute preshow at a base CPM of around €75. Hagedorn’s bet is that exhibitors can bring cinema into the digital ad market while keeping the premium pricing newspapers gave away when they went programmatic.

His company, called Cinemataztic, gives buyers two ways to purchase inventory: first, agencies already running programmatic campaigns can reach the inventory through supply-side platforms and demand-side platforms such as Smartclip Google’s DV360 respectively, which lets cinema sit inside budgets that were going to be spread across other channels anyway, Advertisers who would rather skip the intermediary fees can book directly through Hagedorn’s own interface.

Either way, the buyer can pick what individual film they want to target, so a brand could run only against the upcoming Avengers: Doomsday movie if it likes. The impressions they buy come straight from exhibitors’ ticketing systems, which means one ticket sold counts as one impression. There are “no multipliers here,” said Hagedorn, a dig at the audience multipliers that inflate numbers elsewhere in media.

There’s also a performance bent to the inventory. Audiences log into a pre-show game, which feeds real-time targeting data and lets advertisers push vouchers to their phones. Hagedorn pointed to a Specsavers campaign as an example. On lead generation campaigns, he claimed an average click-through rate of 10%, a figure display buyers stopped dreaming about years ago.

So far a lot of the money has come from digital agencies edging up the funnel into brand work. Hagedorn said he has seen little cannibalisation of advertisers still buying cinema the old way. He said the business has grown 50% a year and forecasts the same for next year, when the company expands into Sweden, Thailand and a third, bigger market he wouldn’t share.

Even so, the programmatication of cinema will be a slow burn. Advertising is still its third-largest revenue line after tickets and concessions. Most hand it entirely to sales houses on contracts of three to five years and only learn that demand is falling when the next guarantee shrinks. Hagedorn has been warning them about this for years. “They cannot stay offline forever,” he said.

Brands that gatecrash cultural moments tend to stick to tournaments and royal weddings, and they rarely pick a game that lets players mug strangers in a fake Florida. Grand Theft Auto, the culture-warping franchise, is the exception, and its sixth installment is drawing the kind of guerilla marketing normally reserved for FIFA and the Olympics.

Anyone trying to keep up can follow the running tally at GTA6Brands.com, an independent tracker run by Mike Litman that logs a new stunt each day for his GTA 6 Marketing News podcast. Hardly anything in culture still sets off that kind of scramble across so many brands at once, and with GTA the explanation is scale.

“I’ve tracked over 290 brand moves around GTA 6 so far, including around 130 unofficial activations,” said Litman. “That’s a remarkable amount of marketing activity orbiting a product launch, particularly when so many of those brands have no formal relationship with Rockstar. What’s interesting is the range of brands getting involved.”

With media this fragmented, marketers have a shortlist of moments when a mass audience shows up at the same time, and most of those moments involve a ball. GTA earned its spot on that list over 13 years. When GTA 5 launched in 2013 it made $1 billion in three days, which owner Take Two called a rate faster than any other video game, film or other entertainment product has ever managed. For context, Marvel’s “The Avengers” movie took 19 days to reach that number. In the years since it launched, the game has since surpassed 230 million units sold worldwide across three console generations. The wait for the sequel turned into an event of its own. GTA 6’s second trailer received 475 million views across all platforms within 24 hours of its release, beating the 365 million in 24 hours for Marvel’s Deadpool & Wolverine film. Take Two has issued $8 billion for its 2027 guidance for the fiscal year the game launches in. For a brand that wants a crowd all watching the same thing at once, that’s about as close to a World Cup final as entertainment gets, and nobody has to get past FIFA’s lawyers.

“I think the fascinating thing is that GTA 6 has achieved something usually reserved for the World Cup or Super Bowl, but without the stadium, the broadcast rights or the traditional sponsorship machinery,” said Litman. “It’s become a cultural event in its own right. And brands aren’t waiting for permission to participate.”

A few did get permission, though. Rockstar’s list of official partners is short and made up mostly of platforms. Sony signed an exclusive marketing deal, which put a “Plays Best on PS5” tag on the game’s official channels. Netflix got the “Extended Look” trailer six trailers before YouTube in August, in what Take Two boss Strauss Zelnick called a “groundbreaking partnership”. YouTube is running a 24-hour launch live livestream from its own channel, and the Miami Heat have redesigned court, custom uniforms and Vice City merchandise.

“Nike is going back to its roots and what built the brand: cultural relevance through people who have the trust of a community. It is a bold choice when most advertising pitches are about AI. Nike uses AI too, as a tool, but clearly shows it understands that what stays scarce is trust. Trust comes from identity and proximity: who is speaking, and how close they are to the community listening. It also shows what creators and athletes must build to ask a brand like Nike for more than just a campaign fee. Most of them are offering access to an audience that belongs to a social or tech platform, targeted and measured with the data that the platform makes available. Global stars aside, only those who directly own the relationship with their audience and their data can negotiate a share of the long-term value.”

— Alessandro De Zanche, founder of media consultancy ADZ Strategies on Nike’s bet on creators to drive growth.

#7255. D2B: Credit Analyst - Dayshift Remote

Position: Credit Analyst - Dayshift Remote

Salary: Php 65,000 to Php 90,000/ monthly (negotiable based on experience)

Working Hours & Conditions: Monday to Friday, 9:00 AM – 5:00 PM AEST/AEDT, Melbourne Time and Australian Holidays

Employment: Full Time - Independent Contractor

The company is an Australian mortgage broking business dedicated to helping clients secure tailored lending solutions with clarity and confidence. The firm partners closely with an extensive panel of major and non-major lenders to support clients across residential home loans, refinancing, and specialized debt structures. Driven by rigorous compliance standards, operational efficiency, and high-touch service, the company delivers a seamless, transparent borrowing experience from initial discovery through to settlement and ongoing portfolio management.

The company is seeking an exceptional, highly analytical Credit Analyst to take operational ownership of the credit assessment and deal structuring lifecycle. Partnering directly with the Principal Broker and collaborating alongside our in-house Loan Processor, you will step in immediately following the client interview via a warm handover to drive applications from initial analysis through to formal approval and settlement.

This role requires deep expertise across complex residential lending scenarios (PAYG, self-employed, trust, and SMSF), rigorous hands-on proficiency in Connective Mercury and Quickli, and a robust compliance mindset to safeguard quality assurance across every file.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.

#7253. How Jell-O’s new creative platform looks to shift consumer perceptions

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The campaign, the first by Johannes Leonardo for the Kraft Heinz brand, represents its largest marketing investment in more than a decade.

Jell-O on Monday launched a new creative platform, “Oh Hello, Jell-O,” that seeks to shift consumer perceptions about how the product can be enjoyed beyond traditional use cases. The effort represents the Kraft Heinz brand’s largest marketing investment in more than a decade, per details shared with Marketing Dive.

“As a nearly 130-year-old brand, we recognized a disconnect between how familiar consumers are with Jell-O and how often they think to purchase it,” Kathryn O’Brien, head of marketing for desserts at Kraft Heinz, said in emailed comments. “We want Jell-O to be more than something that comes to mind for a bowl of gelatin or pudding, but rather a part of the creative process in the kitchen.”

Central to the campaign are two 15-second spots that show home cooks in monochrome kitchens preparing desserts with Jell-O before a social-inspired flip transition pivots the scene to others marveling over the finished product.

The campaign was created by Johannes Leonardo and is the first campaign for the brand by the agency, which has held creative duties on select Kraft Heinz brands since 2020. The spots were directed by Drew Kirsch, a veteran of music videos who has worked with Taylor Swift, and feature an original electronic-pop song that uses “oh hello, Jell-O” as a vocal hook.

“Rather than borrowing an existing song, we saw an opportunity to build an ownable sonic identity around the platform,” O’Brien said. “The original track is a recognizable brand asset that can extend across social, content and future campaign moments.”

As part of the launch, Jell-O will take over the Las Vegas Sphere this week, transforming the orb into a gelatin mold, a “dirt cup” dessert and banana cream pudding. “Oh Hello, Jell-O” will run across streaming, online video, social and digital throughout 2027, with investment pulsing around key seasonal moments. The significant media investment comes at a pivotal moment for the brand, O’Brien explained.

“Our first-ever CTV buy allows us to bring the campaign's highly visual, music-driven creative to life on the big screen, showcasing the playfulness, vibrant colors and unexpected transformations in a high-attention environment,” the executive said. “We're taking an integrated approach throughout 2027, combining streaming and online video with social, digital and e-commerce touchpoints.”

The creative platform builds on a 2023 rebrand, crafted with agency Brand Opus, that updated Jell-O’s visual identity with a more modern look and feel. The rebrand, which at the time was its first such effort in 10 years, arrived amid an industry-wide move to help CPG brands engage with new consumer groups.

Kraft Heinz in February stopped plans to split its condiment and grocery staples businesses, with CEO Steve Cahillane saying many of the CPG company’s challenges are fixable. The company is pouring $700 million into a turnaround strategy that includes significant investments in marketing, which has been increased to at least 6% of net sales.

While Kraft Heinz saw organic net sales decrease 1.1% in Q3 2026, the company’s market share, sales and volume trends improved in every region, per an earnings report. Jell-O was cited on an earnings call as a brand with which the company is looking to win share by investing selectively.

#7251. Media Buying Briefing: The midterm elections offer a window into media buying for ’28

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Buying Briefing covers the latest in agency news and media buying for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Monday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

So many surprises came out of the midterm elections, and only one of them was the rout by Democrats over Republicans on Nov. 3 and in the days following.

There’s a lot to be unpacked as to what impact the $12.06 billion spent during the 2025-2026 election cycle had, according to AdImpact’s revised numbers post-election, but let’s start with the fact that the outlay was the most of any cycle on record and $460 million (4%) more than AdImpact’s last projection in June.

It’s also the first time a midterm election outspent a presidential election — which only portends even more to be spent in 2028’s presidential race.

Why did Republicans fare so poorly when they outspent Democrat candidates overall? According to AdImpact, Republican candidates dropped $5.51 billion, a 27% hike over the 2024 cycle, compared to Dems’ $5.11 billion, which actually represented a 4% drop.

In light of that, it’s fair to ask whether political advertising still delivers the same impact and effect on voters as it had in a simpler media environment. More specifically, is TV still the way to influence voters? The answer leans toward no, since TV still attracted the most dollars, with both broadcast and streaming growing their ad dollars.

“Reaching voters and engaging them are two different things. Ads build awareness. On their own, they don’t persuade anyone or get them to the polls,” said Travis Trawick, CEO of FullPAC, a nonpartisan political technology firm. “The shift is from talking at voters to talking with them. The technology exists today to have two-way conversations at scale, see what’s landing and feed it straight back into strategy while there’s still time to act on it.”

The numbers generally support that tech-forward approach. Looking again at post-election AdImpact data, broadcast TV set a record $5.85 billion, up 9% from 2024, as its share of spend edged up from 48% to 49%. CTV rose 14% to $2.66 billion, while digital media rose 11% to $1.91 billion. Media losing share included cable (−7%), radio (−13%), and satellite (−7%).

But what about the hottest thing in media buying right now, creators? Political candidates turned to them heavily in this cycle. New research from creator marketing firm Kyra, which works with SharkNinja, H&M and L’Oréal to run influencer campaigns across TikTok, Instagram and YouTube, showed a strong swing in influence for Democratic-leaning creators. D-leaning creator posts drew 329.9 million views from 923 posts by 146 creators, while Republican-leaning ones drew 37 million from 170 posts by 27 creators — about 9 to 1 in views and 5 to 1 in posts.

(One important caveat that Kyra noted in its research: its YouTube data is “thin [with 9.9 million views], and we don’t cover X, podcasts, Rumble or long-form YouTube, where right-leaning creators are widely thought to be stronger.”)

Political ad tech firms are noticing that influence. “When you can have trusted messengers really resonating with an audience, whether you’re trying to sell a product or convince someone to vote for a candidate, having that trusted messenger is really a powerful thing,” explained Mark Jablonowski, CEO of DSPolitical, an ad-tech firm that works with candidates mostly on the Democratic side of the aisle. “Having the social sphere of people responding to the news and clipping and sharing in a very organic and viral way is an important thing.”

Jablonowski said Republicans have historically utilized the MAGA network of influencers much more effectively in the past. “This was one of the first years that Democrats really spent significant resources and time on focusing on this, and it was definitely a component of their success,” he said, although he argued that the critical issues of broken promises, war and inflation had the single largest effect on the voter swing —far more than any media strategy had.

There’s little doubt even more ad dollars will be poured into the next presidential election than we witnessed this year. And it will start sooner than ever, with 2027 possibly enjoying more of a windfall of primary spending than any off-year, said Jablonowski. He cited local down ballot races as contributing to that expected increase.

“When we’re looking to 2028, we’re going to see more of these advanced technologies being attainable by the down ballot races,” he said. “You’re just going to see all of the local races really utilizing this best in class tech because it is available and consumable.”

FullPAC’s Trawick agreed that 2028 will break all the records that have been set before in every election cycle, presidential or midterm. “The question is whether campaigns keep spending the same way,” he saod. “They shouldn’t. 2027 and 2028 call for a different approach, and it starts with the calendar. The campaigns that win will plan earlier: introduce the candidate, build relationships and test messages long before broadcast rates spike.”

As far as preferred media, Jablonowski expects to see not only more CTV/streaming and creator investment by candidates, but also digital audio/streaming. “The plumbing has finally been plumbed in, where you can address audiences at scale through streaming audio, whether it be podcast or or music,” he said.

StackAdapt’s political team, in an email to Digiday, said its expects well-funded presidential candidates could start buying ads to build name recognition as early as Q2 or Q3 next year, depending on when they enter the race.

Expect to see TV, particularly linear, used less and in different ways. “Money follows what can be measured. We can expect to see more spending on channels that reach a specific voter and show you whether it worked: texting, CTV and streaming and targeted digital,” said Trawick. “Broadcast won’t disappear, but late-cycle saturation gets harder to justify. Three weeks of TV at peak rates, aimed at people who already voted early, is the first line item that should get a hard look. The bigger shift is the calendar, not the channel. Early voting turned election day into a season, and budgets have to be built for the whole season.”

There’s really only one set of numbers that was important last week: sales of Grand Theft Auto’s 6th installment. According to Sensor Tower (numbers that have not yet been confirmed by Rockstar Games) pre-orders and first-day sales exceeded 24.5 million copies, generating approximately $2.36 billion. It’s widely considered one of the biggest entertainment blockbusters in any medium ever. So, if your brand isn’t too worried about brand safety or adjacency to hardcore content, maybe GTA 7, whenever that comes out, is the place to be.

“John was one of the rare ones that could negotiate an entire marketplace — including sports, and he knew everything about the sports marketplace — but didn’t have to just be a specialist … with John leaving the business, we are going to be missing one of the true intellectual hearts of the buying side.”

Meta’s reported CTV expansion and AppLovin’s alleged interest in Criteo are foreboding for independent ad tech as major platforms extend their reach.

HUMAN Security’s latest investigation queries the effectiveness of CTV verification safeguards.

Advertisers continue to increase investments on the retail weekend but AI, tariffs and discounting pressure present banana skins.

You will negotiate directly, build the playbooks and workflows that let the business self-serve on low-risk deals, and escalate the right issues to Legal at the right time. Build and scale vendor contracting operations

A track record of negotiating directly with vendors and their counsel, independently and at pace.

Experience designing contracting processes that are consistent, transparent, and easy for the business to follow.

Clear writing and speaking, with the ability to explain legal and commercial risk plainly and the judgment to know when to hold a position, approve a reasonable risk, or escalate.

Comfort using AI tools, Claude in particular, with sound judgment about when to rely on outputs, how to verify them, and how to protect confidential information.

A degree in a relevant field (for example, law, economics, or political science), though we value experience over the specific degree.

Sell-side experience negotiating customer, partner, or reseller agreements for a SaaS or technology company, which helps you see vendor terms from the other side.

Hands-on experience with Ironclad or a similar CLM tool (Juro, Docusign CLM) or building AI-assisted legal workflows, ideally with some exposure to AI governance.

Experience working across jurisdictions or with distributed, international teams.

Experience at a fast-growing, developer-focused, cloud, security, or open source company.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7248. Hybrid won the in-housing debate, at least among the largest advertisers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Reports of the media agency’s death remain greatly exaggerated. Its job description, however, is another story.

Advertisers are carving up the media operation with more intent, keeping the work that sits closest to the brand and handing the sprawling specialist stuff back to their agencies.

The shift has been playing out in fits and starts for the best part of a decade. COMvergence’s first Global Advertiser Survey shows how far its got. The media agency research firm polled 28 senior marketing, media, procurement and operations leaders at 21 global advertisers, who between them control more than $22 billion in media spend. A quarter of them buy or control media in-house. Of the rest, around a third are thinking about bringing spend in-house over the next 12 months.

“It’s a bit like a football club,” said Jon Williams, founder and chief creative officer at Freedom Works, a collective of senior agency execs who help brands build in-house teams. “You build your own academy and you’re proud of it. You still buy a striker when you need goals. You just don’t keep a dozen agents on the payroll in case.”

Whether they’ve started in-housing or are still weighing it up, few advertisers want to take everything off their agency’s plate. Most would rather divide the work. Nearly 80% of respondents described their setup as hybrid, mixing internal teams with agency support. Fewer than 18% run media entirely in-house.

“The CMO isn’t always the one pushing,” said Scott Grenz, head of U.S. operations for COMvergence. “Much of the energy comes from internal media, procurement and data/analytics teams. They believe owning their data and getting full transparency are key to media effectiveness, and that often leads to bringing parts of the digital media scope in-house.

“Agencies have become more transparent, and advertisers are looking at their media ecosystem as a whole, asking how best to use all their resources, internal and external, to hit their critical KPIs, said Grenz.

Which is to say the motive for in-housing has moved from saving money to getting better at specific things. Mars is staffing internal teams to get ready for agentic shopping, while Gap has gone as far as setting up its own entertainment studio. Lego brought programmatic in-house partly so it could make better informed calls on which ad tech vendors to use. Its agency, and its vendors, can expect sharper questions as a result.

“When I started out, the in-house studio was where ads went to get resized,” said Williams. “The agency did the thinking and the studio did the cutdowns. Nobody would say that now.”

Getting here took the best part of a decade. For most of that time, in-housing was a cost and transparency play with advertisers chasing cheaper media and a clearer view of where the money went. The ANA’s 2016 report on agency rebates gave plenty of them reason to look. Somewhere along the way, the question moved on to what their own teams could do faster or better.

In-house teams tend to go where the budgets are, which for most advertisers means digital. The largest group of respondents put 60% to 70% of total media spend into digital and plenty go higher, according to the report. Only a very small minority keep digital below 30%.

Unsurprisingly, social media is where advertisers are most willing to take the wheel. More than 85% of the in house operators in the survey run social themselves. Search display and programmatic trail at roughly 57%. The logic is simple enough. Social rewards fast decisions and direct access to audience data, both of which come easier when the team sits inside the business.

The same can’t be said of retail media, CTV and influencer marketing. They remain mostly agency-run, with relatively few advertisers taking them on directly. All three are fragmented and specialized enough to keep agencies in demand. Take Retail media. Its sprawl of networks and measurement approaches is the sort of mess advertisers seem happy to pay someone else to untangle. Several respondents still named influencer, commerce and retail media as where they’re experimenting with new models or looking for outside guidance.

“Building an in-house media practice is hard. It carries real risk, takes significant resources and requires capabilities many advertisers don’t have yet,” said Grenz.” Still, there are plenty of use cases across the industry, from big advertisers and small, and no single model has won out. Our data shows that many advertisers who have in-housed still work with a media agency. That’s the real takeaway: it isn’t all or nothing. Advertisers are working out the right mix of internal and external expertise to make their media effective.”

For all the talk of brands doing more on their own, agency rosters remain crowded. More than half (54) of the marketers COMvergence interviewed use more than one media agency and more than half bring in digital specialist agencies alongside their main one.

Still, that crowding probably won’t last. More than 80% of the advertisers working with multiple agencies plan to simplify or consolidate. They want specialist expertise and fewer partners at the same time — a tension that should keep the pitch consultants busy for a while.

“Some of it is procurement,” said Williams. “One in-house lead told us it took nine months to get a new supplier set up. Do that ten times and you’ll consolidate too. And AI is taking away a lot of the everyday work that used to keep four or five agencies busy.”

Half of the respondents use global hubs, offshore resources or both. Their comments pointed to India, Colombia, Brazil, Mexico and other regional centers, mostly handling digital operations, programmatic buying, social execution and support work.

Advertisers see these setups as agency-run rather than as their own teams. That’s a handy calling card for holdcos selling scale. In fact, COMvergence expects hubs and offshore talent to become an even bigger differentiator for agencies as labor costs rise and more execution gets automated.

“There’s no universal ceiling,” said Grenz. “It comes down to how each advertiser weighs the risk and reward of in-housing against agency management. Agencies have made a strong case that they’re a critical partner, and they’ve shown they’re willing to work within whatever hybrid model a client wants.”

Asked what they’d like more of, respondents pointed squarely at agency operating models, which made up more than 40% of all feedback. They want to see how peers structure internal teams, split work between brand and agency, govern media investment and run in-house functions.

The usual caveats apply to a report like this. Twenty one advertisers is a small sample so a handful of companies can swing a percentage by a lot. COMvergence plans to run the survey every year and the second edition should say more about the direction of travel than this first snapshot can.

Even so, the findings are a good encapsulation of how senior marketers at the largest advertisers see where things are headed.

Meta’s reported CTV expansion and AppLovin’s alleged interest in Criteo are foreboding for independent ad tech as major platforms extend their reach.

#7247. Philadelphia whips up bigger holiday marketing spend amid brand revamp

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Brand character Phillyboy returns in a Santa-like role to dish out blocks of cream cheese to be used in cheesecake recipes.

“Really Philly Good,” which debuted in February, was developed to broaden consumer perceptions of what Philadelphia has to offer. Rather than only showing the product as a cold spread for bagels and snacks, the platform highlights how cream cheese can be used to enrich more involved meals. A larger marketing push around Q4 responds to the insight that one-third of Philadelphia cream cheese bricks are sold during the November-December period and Google search trends data that show spikes in interest for cheesecake recipes during the season.

In the new work, Phillyboy acts as a Santa-like figure, trotting into houses saddled on his dairy cow to dole out blocks of cream cheese that are then used to make cheesecake. The rugged Western-themed character is accompanied by a spin on Christmas standards like “Jingle Bells.” Agency Johannes Leonardo is behind “Really Philly Good” while Nalle Sjöblad, from Spark & Riot, directed the holiday ad spots. The full media plan spans TV, streaming, connected TV, online video, social, creators and retail elements.

Beyond video advertising, Philadelphia is running a national integration with “Happy Hour with Ina Garten” that is supported by sponsored social content and a custom cheesecake recipe on the Barefoot Contessa website and Substack. Other social and digital activations include a cheesecake-building experience on Pinterest, an augmented reality Snapchat lens and a focus on TikTok search and trending food and drink Reels content on Meta.

In addition, the Kraft Heinz marketer enlisted TikTok and Instagram creators, including Tineke “Tini” Younger and Kiley O’Donnell, for holiday cheesecake content. The creator program started on Nov. 2 and has so far drawn nearly 1 million organic views and 43,000 engagements, the brand said.

Kraft Heinz is broadly revamping its portfolio as it tries to win back consumers who have shifted toward protein-rich and better-for-you offerings. In August, it announced plans to spend an additional $100 million on its turnaround, with the lion’s share of the investment concentrated in marketing. Marketing will represent at least 6% of the company’s net sales this year.

Jell-O, another brand under Kraft Heinz, unveiled a new creative platform on Monday that is backed by its largest marketing investment in more than a decade. Johannes Leonardo also handled “Oh Hello, Jell-O,” the shop’s first work for the brand.

Frontline Wildfire Defense is the fastest-growing company in the wildfire protection industry, serving as the premier manufacturer and installer of exterior wildfire defense systems for residential and commercial structures. As wildfires rapidly intensify across the West, they threaten communities and destabilize property insurance markets—creating a vital need and a powerful market opportunity for proven structural defense solutions. Beyond our core technology, Frontline is strategically positioning itself to capture high-growth adjacent opportunities, including strategic insurance partnerships and municipal fire service integration.

We welcome inquiries from qualified professionals seeking to contribute to a high-growth enterprise dedicated to preserving lives and property.

Frontline is seeking a strategic, operationally minded Chief Financial Officer to lead our finance function and help drive the company’s next phase of growth. Frontline is scaling quickly, and wildfire risk is reshaping the availability and affordability of property insurance across the West.  This creates a large market for effective structure defense, along with strategic opportunities in the insurance sector, recurring-revenue services, and fire department and public-sector partnerships.

As a member of the executive team, you will partner closely with the CEO, COO, leadership team, and Board to turn strategic priorities into clear plans and measurable results. You will bring financial rigor, operational discipline, and a commercial mindset to company-wide decisions, and you will help identify, evaluate, and execute new growth opportunities, including strategic insurance partnerships and financial offerings.

This role reports directly to the CEO and leads the existing finance organization, as well as Frontline’s legal function.

We’re looking for a finance leader who has helped a company through this kind of growth before, and who can build the foundation for our next stage, including a potential IPO and/or other strategic growth opportunities.

#7242. Brands boost Black Friday CTV, retail and search spending

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Brands are expected to spend 10-30% more on digital media activity over Black Friday and Cyber Monday, compared with 2025, according to five media buyers who spoke with Digiday. Retail media, particularly Amazon, search, CTV and YouTube, are expected to soak up much of that increase.

Spending growth around Black Friday (Nov. 27) and Cyber Monday (Nov. 30), the twin peaks of the holiday season for many retailers, hardly comes as a surprise. According to Triple Whale, 33,000 U.S. brands spent $607 million across the Black Friday/Cyber Monday period last year; this year, Bain Capital estimates U.S. companies to record $85 billion in sales over the five day period, accounting for 8.5% of total holiday spending for 2026.

But there’s nuance to be considered, especially with retailers choosing to discount earlier in the year.

“We expect higher spend across direct-response channels this year, though it varies by category and brand,” said Brett Fischer, associate director of performance media at e-commerce-focused media agency Collective Measures.

For one, product discounting and marketing activity kicked off earlier this year. According to commerce media company Fluent, 32% of shoppers had already begun holiday purchases prior to Black Friday.

Meanwhile, the number of products listed at a discount between Oct. 1-20 rose 56%, and increased 71% during the week preceding Prime Big Deal Days, according to data from e-commerce company Wayvia. In 2025, the same discounting surge didn’t kick in until the middle of November.

That’s driven budget increases, or forced some marketers to spread their ad dollars over a longer period of time. Nola Ladd, associate director of brand media at Collective Measures, said the agency’s clients had spread their ad dollars thinner over a longer period, with the aim of improving consumer consideration.

“Mid-funnel investment is running roughly flat year over year,” said Ladd. “Instead of growing it in this period, we’ve spread it across more of the year so brands are building consideration well before peak season.”

U.S. retail sales are expected to rise 4.2% this year compared with 2025, according to Oxford Economics, while the National Retail Federation (NRF) estimated that 182.7 million Americans will shop during the five-day run of discounts and sales, a slight reduction from 2025’s 186.9 million.

For some retailers, heavy discounting in the run-up to Black Friday is beginning to bite. Data from media agency PMG found that weekly sales rose 10% during October’s Prime Big Deal Days (staged by Amazon on Oct. 6-7), with grocery purchases increasing 37.7% in volume. But the average selling price in that category fell 13.1%, while electronics revenues were “flat.”

“Many of our clients in CPG and home delivery are watching sell-through closely,” said Scott Shamberg, president and CEO at media agency Mile Marker.

“Many of our clients saw softness for the last few weeks but are still expecting to see demand and sales pick up,” said Michelle Merklin, vp of search innovation and growth, Tinuiti.

Companies affected by tariff-related cost increases will have to choose between crucial profit margin or the need to match competitors’ discounts. “It’s important for brands to continue to stay competitive within this time frame,” said Carly Carson, head of integrated media at PMG.

“Our clients’ spend this Black Friday and Cyber Monday is mixed — some are flat and some are up. There has been some softening in the market the last quarter, so some clients are being conservative,” said Josy Amann, co-founder, Ars X Machina (AXM).

Per PMG data, brands that discounted across a larger range of their portfolio enjoyed a lot more growth during October: on average, those with 75% of their product set on discount saw 16% overall revenue growth, while companies that discounted 25% or less of their products saw overall revenues fall 11%.

Sales softness, and increased cost of sales hasn’t led to clients risking a retreat from Black Friday spending — the weekend window is too important a sales event for retailers. “We have definitely been making strong recommendations around maintaining a full-funnel strategy… BFCM is a great time to find new consumers in a shopping mindset,” said Amann.

But some advertisers were shifting dollars into channels considered to be safer bets. “Less brand, more performance channels,” Shamberg explained. Merklin also said Tinuiti clients were, on the whole, choosing not to cut media budgets.

Those safer bets include CTV and YouTube, as well as retail media options. According to Brock Berry, CEO of digital ad trafficking platform AdCellerant, the quantity of advertisers running CTV activity during November was 25% than the same period last year, despite CPMs running as much as 18% higher in some regions following high competition from political spenders during the midterm elections. Shamberg said he expected to see “double-digit growth” in each of those channels among Mile Marker clients.

At the same time, product discovery behaviors are under question. A survey of 29,000 consumers commissioned by delivery company DHL estimated that 90% of shoppers used generative AI chatbots or assistants to compare prices, while half said they used the tools to browse.

As such, marketers are paying close attention to search, Merklin noted. She said Tinuiti clients were expected to spend 11 to 15% more with Google compared with last year, with total search spending up 11% versus 2025. Clients’ standalone display campaign spending had largely shifted into the tech giant’s Demand Gen and Performance Max campaigns, which rose 30% and both include display and video inventory.

PMG’s Carson said clients were spending on social as well, with an eye to influencing product discovery, while also increasing CTV investments. “That’s an area where we expect investment to be up well into the double digits year-over-year,” she said.

“[It’s] something I don’t see slowing down in 2027,” addedCarson. “More advanced targeting capabilities here, including around placing ads during specific content contexts, are going to make it easier for brands to further invest.”

Meta’s reported CTV expansion and AppLovin’s alleged interest in Criteo are foreboding for independent ad tech as major platforms extend their reach.

HUMAN Security’s latest investigation queries the effectiveness of CTV verification safeguards.

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agency of record appointment includes a mandate to lean into the QSR’s heritage and forward-looking initiatives like a Burger Revolution strategy.

Carl’s Jr. has appointed David&Goliath as marketing agency of record as it shifts focus to cooked-to-order burgers and tries to revive a bolder, boundary-pushing brand personality, according to news shared with Marketing Dive. Carl’s Jr. did not previously have a dedicated AOR, and instead worked with agencies on a project basis, Marketing Dive understands.

David&Goliath, which is part of Innocean Worldwide, will oversee brand strategy, creative development, experiential marketing, production and storytelling for the fast-food chain, with a mandate to lean into Carl’s Jr.'s heritage while shoring up cultural relevance with younger consumers. Its first work is expected to arrive in Q1 of next year.

The announcement was accompanied by the release of a limited-time Angus Maximus 5.7-ounce burger. The changes are part of a larger effort by Carl’s Jr. to emphasize food quality, which has manifested in promotional areas like limited-time offerings.

Other fast-food companies, including rival Burger King, have thrown a spotlight on premium ingredients and restaurant improvements to win back price-sensitive diners. Carl’s Jr.’s franchised store count has declined in recent years. Franchisees of the brand and sister restaurant Hardee’s have faced their own profitability struggles, with a major one in Calirfornia in April declaring bankruptcy, Marketing Dive’s sister publication Restaurant Dive previously reported.

Carl’s Jr. has made internal changes to its marketing leadership this year as well. She credited David&Goliath with possessing the bravery necessary to build on Carl’s Jr.’s legacy.

“Together, we’re excited to push boundaries, expand our connection with customers, and bring the unmistakable Carl’s Jr. spirit to a new generation of burger lovers,” Diemer said in a press statement.

Carl’s Jr.’s advertising has not shied away from pushing buttons in the past. The chain in the 2000s became known for TV commercials featuring bikini-clad models suggestively eating burgers. It has dipped back into the racy well in recent years, including through a 2025 campaign with influencer Alix Earle that touted offerings like a hangover-themed burger.

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Gap Inc. posted its earnings results for the third quarter of 2026 on Thursday, with double-digit growth from the Gap brand offsetting declines from Athleta and Banana Republic.

#7234. Intuit offloads Mailchimp to Bending Spoons to deepen bets on AI

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Bending Spoons, a technology conglomerate whose portfolio includes AOL and Eventbrite, will acquire the email automation company for $3.68 billion.

Intuit has entered into an agreement to sell email automation business Mailchimp to technology conglomerate Bending Spoons for $3.68 billion, the company confirmed in its latest earnings report. Intuit acquired Mailchimp in 2021 for $12 billion.

Intuit’s decision to sell comes as Mailchimp continues to be a drag on its business, which includes financial services QuickBooks, TurboTax and Credit Karma. The move is also positioned as a way to sharpen Intuit's focus on artificial intelligence and its other core products.

“We have built AI-native experiences to help businesses win new customers, manage their pipeline and help them grow through our lead-to-cash capabilities,” said Sasan Goodarzi, CEO of Intuit, on a call discussing the company’s latest earnings. “Selling Mailchimp sharpens our focus behind that strategy, while positioning Mailchimp and its team for continued success in their next chapter with Bending Spoons.”

Mailchimp, founded in 2001, was once the gem of the email marketing industry, but in the years following its acquisition by Intuit has struggled to maintain growth. Mailchimp’s revenue decreased 1% to $313 million during Q1 of the company’s fiscal 2027 for the period ended Oct. 31, a contrast to Intuit's overall revenue growth of 12% over the same period.

In May, Intuit announced that it was reducing its full-time workforce by 17% and “rightsizing” its investment in Mailchimp. As struggles persisted, the company in August revealed that it started running Mailchimp as a separate operating segment, a move that was intended to offer investors more transparency as Intuit tried to manage the unit for profitability.

The Mailchimp deal is expected to close in early 2027, at which point Bending Spoons plans to invest “substantially” in Mailchimp and build on its existing tools, according to a separate statement. Bending Spoons, an Italian company founded in 2013, acquires businesses and works to improve them with the goal of keeping them long-term. Other businesses acquired by the company include Vimeo, WeTransfer, Eventbrite, Streamyard and AOL.

Bending Spoons went public in July at a value of over $18 billion. Holding company Berkshire Hathaway commands a 4% ownership stake of the company.

Intuit last week launched a campaign — its first work with agency Mother New York — promoting the integration of its Intuit Intelligence agentic platform into QuickBooks. The campaign, “More Brains for your Business,” positions the AI capabilities as a helping hand instead of a replacement for human talent.

#7232. The Rundown: BADBOX 3.0, the CTV ad fraud scheme generating 40 billion bid requests a day

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Last week, HUMAN Security claimed it has disrupted the largest publicly disclosed ad fraud operation by bid-request volume, uncovering a network of infected streaming devices generating up to 40 billion fraudulent ad requests daily.

Dubbed “BADBOX 3.0,” the scheme accounted for more than 4% of CTV traffic observed by HUMAN, using compromised Android TV boxes to impersonate legitimate streaming apps and generate advertising activity without viewers’ knowledge.

The Nov. 18 disclosure raises fresh questions about the effectiveness of CTV ad fraud detection — and how much fraudulent inventory slips through existing safeguards, important gaps for the industry to collectively plug as marketers increasingly spend ad dollars on such channels.

According to HUMAN’s Satori Threat Intelligence team, the situation involved a network of compromised, low-cost Android-based streaming devices running malware designed to generate fraudulent video advertising activity without their owners’ knowledge.

The operation was more than three times larger, by peak daily bid-request volume, than VASTFLUX, previously the largest publicly disclosed scheme measured on that basis.

Researchers linked elements of the malware distribution infrastructure to TXCZ, a Chinese electronics manufacturer, identifying connections involving device software, code repositories and command-and-control servers.

HUMAN also uncovered links to earlier BADBOX operations, including BADBOX 2.0, which was exposed in 2025 and involved more than 10 million compromised devices across 222 countries and territories.

The latest discovery illustrates how fraud operations can reconstitute themselves following earlier disruptions, adapting their infrastructure and techniques to evade detection.

The malware then installed advertising software on infected devices, allowing video commercials to play in hidden windows while users continued watching television or navigating their streaming interfaces.

The resulting advertising requests impersonated legitimate CTV applications, making the inventory appear authentic to downstream buyers.

Some fraudulent requests also originated from residential proxy networks, helping disguise their source as ordinary household internet traffic.

HUMAN identified 11 proxy networks in which compromised devices could be enrolled without their owners’ consent, effectively turning consumer internet connections into infrastructure available for third-party use. Separately, researchers uncovered spoofed server-side ad insertion traffic, in which fraudulent requests purported to represent advertisements inserted into streaming sessions that did not actually exist.

The scale of the operation is considerable, although its financial impact remains unclear.

Crucially, the 40 billion figure represents bid requests, not completed advertising transactions or billable impressions. Consequently, it cannot be used to calculate advertiser losses without establishing how much fraudulent inventory was purchased.

Hence, the question remains: what proportion of the 40 billion daily bid requests became paid impressions? And how much money was spent on invalid inventory?

However, due to the intricacies of online ad auctions – where CPMs can vary significantly – HUMAN was unable to provide an exact dollar amount when asked about the financial impact of the operation.

Nonetheless, sources approached by Digiday estimated that BADBOX 3.0 could potentially have cost advertisers millions of dollars per day at its height.

Shailin Dhar, partner at Futureproof TMT, cautioned against equating the operation’s 40 billion daily bid requests with actual advertiser losses, noting that CTV supply audited by his firm since 2024 has averaged a fill rate of approximately 2%. He further counseled that fraudulent inventory originating from low-end applications and devices could perform considerably worse.

At this juncture, it’s worth considering that enormous volumes of fraudulent auction traffic do not necessarily translate into equivalent volumes of wasted advertising expenditure. At an illustrative fill rate of 0.5% and realized revenue of $3 per thousand impressions, 40 billion daily requests would generate approximately $600,000 in revenue. However, Dhar stressed that such calculations should not be mistaken for evidence of the operation’s actual earnings.

Nevertheless, the operation’s ability to masquerade as legitimate streaming inventory highlights potential weaknesses in how CTV advertising transactions are authenticated.

CTV has become an increasingly important channel for advertisers seeking to reach audiences migrating from traditional television. However, its programmatic infrastructure also presents opportunities for fraudsters to exploit fragmented supply paths and inconsistent verification practices.

BADBOX 3.0 demonstrates how apparently legitimate app identifiers, residential IP addresses and video ad requests can be combined to manufacture the appearance of genuine viewing activity. The question is whether existing safeguards can consistently identify such behavior before advertisers are charged.

Futureproof’s Dhar questioned the effectiveness of existing verification safeguards, arguing that private marketplaces and curated deals remain vulnerable because of limited device, content, and connection signals, alongside excessive confidence in identity matching.

“My personal estimate is roughly a 90% chance that at least one comparable operation is running without public identification,” he told Digiday, while acknowledging that its scale and financial impact remain unknown.

Per Dhar’s assessment, effective CTV verification requires persistent checks throughout the advertising supply chain, rather than relying exclusively on pre-bid or post-bid detection.

For now, HUMAN says customers using its Ad Fraud Defense service are protected against the operation’s associated attacks, while its researchers continue monitoring the perpetrators for further adaptations.

The findings come roughly a month after a separate investigation by ad security firm Confiant and researchers at Citizen Lab exposed how online advertising infrastructure was used to distribute spyware, highlighting another vulnerability in the digital advertising ecosystem.

That investigation, detailed in research published by Citizen Lab, found that Israeli company Discover-Tech exploited targeted advertising mechanisms to deliver zero-click spyware, including attacks against devices at Al Jazeera.

While the two operations differed in their objectives — one targeting advertising budgets, the other facilitating surveillance — both illustrate how infrastructure designed to connect advertisers with audiences can be repurposed for malicious activity.

The broader concern is that BADBOX 3.0 is another iteration of an established threat rather than an isolated incident — and that dismantling one operation does not necessarily eliminate the infrastructure or incentives behind the next.

Meta’s reported CTV expansion and AppLovin’s alleged interest in Criteo are foreboding for independent ad tech as major platforms extend their reach.

Advertisers continue to increase investments on the retail weekend but AI, tariffs and discounting pressure present banana skins.

The shift has been playing for the best part of a decade. COMvergence’s first Global Advertiser Survey shows had far its got. T

#7229. Chewy jumps on stop-motion animation trend lighting up holiday ads

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The brand’s research shows nearly 80% of pet owners plan to buy their furry friends at least one present this year.

In an effort to cut through generative artificial intelligence slop this holiday season, several brands have turned to what is considered to be one of the most labor-intensive forms of filmmaking: stop-motion animation. From Levi’s heartwarming claymation spot to Prada’s holiday effort which combines live actors with traditional 2D animation à la “Who Framed Roger Rabbit,” the technique has found itself the medium for many a holiday advertisement this year.

Pet supply company Chewy is no exception. Its holiday campaign marks the first time the brand has used the medium in a national TV spot. Stop-motion animation is used to portray the rich inner lives of pets during the holidays, where a Chewy box transports pets to a world of toys, treats and fun in the snow.

The idea was born out of the long-running Chewy Claus initiative, where owners can write to the company on behalf of their pets. As part of the effort, Chewy will help fund adoptions for shelter animals and send surprises to select letter-writers throughout the month of December.

“It didn't start with stop motion,” said Melissa Bell, Chewy’s chief brand officer, who joined the company in July. “We think about [the holidays] from our point of view, but given the importance of pets and animals for our company in particular, we were like,’ let's start with what our pets and animals think,’ and it naturally blossomed from there.”

Despite ongoing economic concerns potentially putting a damper on gift-giving this year, holiday pet spending continues to be a priority for many, especially for younger consumers. Nearly 80% of pet owners plan on buying a present for their animal this holiday season, with 21% of Gen Z and millennials planning to spend $100 or more, according to data from the brand. Chewy’s efforts go beyond the standard pet food and medication orders to encourage surprising the animals in one’s life and including them in the festive season.

The campaign marks the first time Chewy Claus is getting a national TV spot as part of the holiday campaign, according to Bell.

“The love and following of Chewy Claus is really inspirational, and for us, it was about connecting to…a bigger purpose because it's about helping shelter pets find homes. We wanted to bring that message to a larger audience, and this campaign gives us a way to bring that tradition to more people,” said Bell.

No AI was used in the creation of the spots, according to Bell. The puppets featured in the campaign were all crafted by hand and modeled after real-life animals. In the 30-second spot “A Holiday Dream,” a cat and dog are treated to a world of holiday whimsy created entirely in stop-motion animation. In the 15-second “A Holiday of Treats,” a dog is thrown a treat by a snowman dog toy, which he catches and crashes into a Chewy box filled with toys, before it ends with the dog asleep on the living room floor. Chewy’s in-house brands, such as Frisco, make frequent appearances.

Chewy’s holiday campaign expands beyond the national spot into a social media effort. Influencers, which the brand refers to as “Chewpanions” are central to the strategy and will be promoting the Chewy Claus effort, along with the brand more broadly.

In-person activations are also a key component of the effort and are largely centered around pet adoption. Chewy will host an event in Santa Monica, California, from Dec. 5-6, where attendees can write letters to Chewy Claus, explore a holiday market and meet adoptable pets. A similar event was held in Miami from Nov. 21-22 to kick off the season.

Chewy’s net sales totaled$3.33 billion, a 7.3% year-over-year increase, in its fiscal second quarter ending Aug. 2, per an earnings release. In February, Chewy acquired SmartPak, a horse supply and supplements brand. In May, it expanded its veterinary practice footprint through the acquisition of Modern Animal, bringing its approximate number of locations to 60.

Most Kit creators don't have a dedicated Creator Growth Manager, and when their emails start landing in spam, they need someone who can find the cause quickly and explain it in plain language. We're looking for a Deliverability and Compliance Specialist to own deliverability support for that long tail of creators, run Kit's abuse desk, and make the compliance calls that protect the sending reputation every creator on Kit shares.

You'll also build the playbooks, training, and AI-assisted workflows that help Support and contractors handle more deliverability questions on their own, and you'll partner with our infrastructure engineers as we run more of our sending in-house. This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes digging through headers and bounce logs to find the real cause of a problem, can explain DMARC alignment to a first-time creator without jargon, and is comfortable making a firm call when a situation is gray. You'll report to Alyssa Dulin, Director of Creator Success, and collaborate closely with:

Our Deliverability team: a Deliverability Lead who supports our highest-value accounts and leads deliverability education, and a Deliverability Specialist who onboards and ramps up large senders moving to Kit

Our infrastructure engineers, who run Kit's sending infrastructure

Our compliance and anti-abuse engineers, who build the automated checks that keep spammers off Kit

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

Customer Success Managers (we call them Creator Growth Managers at Kit), Sales and Migrations, and Product Managers

Making account-level compliance or abuse decisions, such as approving, suspending, or closing accounts

Working an abuse desk or abuse mailbox directly: spam and phishing complaints, provider and blocklist reports, and escalations

Supporting senders on shared IP pools at an ESP

Working directly with mailbox provider postmaster teams

Working with creators, digital entrepreneurs, or small businesses

You pick up problems that fall between teams without waiting for permission, and you follow them through until they're closed.
