#7226. D+ Research: Brand’s holiday marketing tactics shift in 2026

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

This is an excerpt from our Digiday+ Research report The 2026 guide to holiday marketing strategies, including A-Frame Brands, Mastercard and Ritual, which examined which commerce channels are dominating marketers’ holiday strategies. The report is based on a survey of 90 brand and retailer professionals, as well as individual interviews with marketing executives.

Digiday’s survey found that brands and retailers plan to adjust the marketing tactics they’ll use during the 2026 holiday season in comparison to those they used in 2025.

The biggest changes are that CTV/streaming and TV ads are gaining traction in 2026, with year-over-year increases of 22 percentage points and 20 percentage points, respectively.

Forty-five percent of survey respondents said they plan to use CTV/streaming ads this holiday season, in comparison to 23% of respondents who said the same in 2025. The 2026 uptick followed a 14 percentage point dip in usage from 2024 to 2025.Meanwhile, marketers’ usage of TV ads remained relatively stable between 2024 and 2025, dropping by only 4 percentage points between 2024 and 2025, but grew by a whopping 20 percentage points in 2026. Thirty-three percent of brands and retailers said they plan to use TV ads this holiday season in comparison to 13% of marketers who said the same in 2025.

“Streaming is a really valid channel to invest in during holiday because consumption of that media spikes,” said Laura Brodie, chief growth officer at supplement company Ritual. “But participating in streaming is actually a decision for the whole year. Starting to engage just for holiday would be a risky move, especially for a smaller brand.”

“If you have the data to back it up over the course of the year showing this is a channel that’s proven and the ROI metrics associated, it can make a lot of sense to spike it during holiday,” she added.

Ari Bloom, founder and CEO of brand incubator A-Frame Brands, said that both streaming and TV ads can be effective for targeting specific demographic groups during the holidays. “Streaming and television is an effective audience conversion mechanism, especially with women over 30 years old,” Bloom said. “We’ve done a lot of work on daytime TV and on QVC. We see significantly bigger segments in the holiday time period from November through mid-December, and we always put attention on those times.”

Last year, brands’ and retailers’ most-used holiday marketing tactic was brand experiences, but that strategy fell to second place in 2026 behind streaming/CTV ads. Forty-one percent of marketers said they expect to use brand experiences as a marketing strategy during the 2026 holiday season in comparison to 50% of marketers who said the same in 2025 — a drop of 9 percentage points.

Brands and retailers are also divesting from using gift guides and influencer hauls/unboxing videos this holiday season. Marketers’ usage of gift guides decreased by 19 percentage points in 2026 vs. 2025, while their use of influencer hauls/unboxing videos fell by 14 percentage points this year in comparison to last year.

Although gift guides and brand experiences decreased in marketer usage for the 2026 holidays, they remain brands’ and retailers’ top performing holiday marketing tactics over time. Thirty-one percent of marketers said gift guides have historically performed the best for their company during the holiday season, while 28% of marketers said the same of brand experiences, putting them in the No. 1 and No. 2 ranking spots, respectively.

Although some marketers are shifting away from using gift guides for the upcoming holiday season, Ritual’s Brodie said gift guides are likely to gain importance in the AI-driven search era because AI-powered search tools often rely on best of lists and curated recommendations to answer consumer queries.

“Gift guides are very interesting in the world of AEO and thinking about LLM optimization,” Brodie said. “Overall, best of lists and gift guides are a close sister to [AI search results]. Gift guides can play an important role in holiday and especially bridging LLM research and AI overviews. So, I’m bullish about gift guides.”

Although brands and retailers are leaning into CTV/streaming ads for the 2026 holidays, those marketing tactics tied in the No. 4 spot for the best-performing holiday marketing tactic over time, while pop-up shops ranked No. 3. Influencer hauls/unboxing videos was the only marketing strategy that fell in both best historic performance and marketers’ intended use during the 2026 holiday season.

A-Frame Brands’ Bloom said brands and retailers should also consider investing in catalogs for the 2026 holiday. “Catalogs have always been very successful, and they’re heavily conversion-focused,” Bloom explained. “When they’re discount-driven and when people’s attention is on buying, you can do offers that you can only get via physical paper, and it does drive people into stores.”

Walmart is allowing access to Meta’s Muse through a gateway that lets it control how AI agents browse, shop and make purchases.

Amazon is paying to reach ChatGPT shoppers while the chatbot is finding other ways to surface its products and deals.

Gap Inc. has tapped boy band Just Your Type (JYT) for a multi-year partnership and launched “The Agency,” a scripted microseries made for Banana Republic.

Get access to tools and analysis to stay ahead of the trends transforming media and marketing

Visit your account page to make changes and renew.

Get Digiday's top stories every morning in your email inbox.

Follow @Digiday for the latest news, insider access to events and more.

#7224. Super Bowl 61: Tracking every ad

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Stay tuned here for the latest marketing moves, sorted by brand, agency and category, as we count down to kickoff on Feb. 14.

Advertiser excitement is already nearing its fever pitch ahead of Super Bowl 61 on Feb. 14, 2027. Disney announced in August that it has sold out of all ad inventory for the game — which for the first time will be played on Valentine’s Day — with a 30-second spot reportedly costing advertisers between $8 million to $9 million.

The big game has attracted new entrants, including Ferrero’s Nutella and Waymo, the provider of driverless robotaxi services owned by Alphabet, along with repeat advertisers including Ritz, Svedka and Cutwater Spirits. The Super Bowl continues to provide advertisers a valuable opportunity to reach consumers, with the 2026 game ranking as the second most-watched Super Bowl in history with an estimated 125.6 million viewers.

Super Bowl 61 will be played at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, California and will air on ABC, ESPN and Disney+. The halftime show, which will be headlined by Jay-Z, is sponsored for a fifth consecutive year by Apple Music.

Marketing Dive will keep this tracker updated with all the brand announcements leading up to the big game — check back for the latest on advertising’s biggest night.

Instacart will return to the Super Bowl for a third consecutive year, the company confirmed. The effort will be handled by Instacart’s in-house creative agency, Local Produce, with support from McCann for a second consecutive year.

Instacart advertised in the 2026 Super Bowl with a 30-second spot, “Bananas,” that starred Ben Stiller and Benson Boone and was centered around a fictional Euro-pop duo called Benissimo. The campaign drove an eight-point spike in new user traffic immediately following the spot, according to the brand. Additionally, the effort scored Instacart three Cannes Lions awards.

“We went from zero to Super Bowl in four years, and since then, our big bets have continued to pay off for the business,” said Instacart CMO Laura Jones.

Mondelēz International cracker brand Ritz will return to the Super Bowl with a 30-second ad airing in the second quarter, per details shared with Marketing Dive. The spot, which marks the brand’s third consecutive appearance, was created by agency 72andSunny and produced by Publicis Production. The commercial is meant to introduce a new creative direction for the brand and will use humor to spotlight Ritz Bitz Cracker Sandwiches and Ritz Drizzled Minis Crackers.

Sazerac’s Svedka Vodka will return to the Super Bowl following its debut during the 2026 game, the brand confirmed. The marketer is turning the effort over to consumers, asking them to submit a 15-second ad featuring its new ready-to-drink Vodka Water cocktail offering.

Consumers can submit their ideas until Dec. 1, and the winner will receive a $500,000 prize. To launch the contest, Svedka linked with Andy Cohen, who will also help evaluate submissions. For its 2026 ad, the marketer used AI to bring its fembot mascot to life, an effort which generated a wave of controversy.

Nutella, the hazelnut and cocoa spread made by Ferrero, will make its debut Super Bowl appearance, per a company announcement. Sister brand Kinder Bueno, which made its debut in the 2026 game, will also return.

Ferrero in October announced that it is committing over $50 million across its portfolio to sports marketing, representing the confectionary giant’s largest individual marketing investment to date.

Ferrero-owned Kinder Bueno will return to the Super Bowl for a second consecutive year, according to a company announcement. The popular confection made its big game debut last year with a campaign that drove a 140% lift in volume sales over the year prior.

The decision to return to the game is in support of a hefty investment by Ferrero in sports. The company announced in October that it is committing over $50 million across its portfolio to its sports strategy, representing its largest individual marketing investment to date.

Waymo, the robotaxi service owned by Google parent Alphabet, is conducting a creative review for its debut Super Bowl appearance, Ad Age reported. The company recently named WPP as its media agency of record for North America, Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

Cutwater, the spirits-based cocktail brand owned by Anheuser-Busch InBev, will return to the Super Bowl for a second year with a national ad, the brand confirmed. The decision is intended to drive further growth for Cutwater, which is currently the fastest growing brand in the total U.S. beverage alcohol industry, per Circana data shared in the company’s announcement.

Cutwater made its Super Bowl debut in 2022 with a 30-second spot called “Here’s to the Lazy Ones” celebrating individuals who have no problem taking a shortcut.

#7222. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.

#7221. Agencies are selling AI efficiency — clients want outcomes

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Amol Waishampayan, co-founder, fullthrottle.ai

Agencies spent the last two years pitching AI to clients as a cost story. Faster work, leaner teams, lower fees. Clients took the meeting, nodded along and then waited for the invoice to shrink. It didn’t. AI strategies now sound interchangeable to clients, who say the savings never showed up in their fees.

That is not an AI problem. It is a positioning problem. Efficiency was the wrong promise. Clients do not hire agencies to be fast. They hire them to grow the business. AI’s real opportunity for agencies is not doing the same work cheaper. It is doing work that actually moves revenue, and proving it.

Agencies are getting operational value from AI. According to the Digiday Media Agency Report 2026, 70% of agencies say AI has cut costs by making tasks more efficient, while 31% say it cost more to implement than expected. But efficiency is a one-time gain.

Once every agency has it, nobody gets paid for it. As one Gartner analyst told Digiday, the honeymoon between agencies and AI is “essentially over.” The agencies that build their AI story on productivity are competing on margin. The ones that build it on performance are competing on value, and value is what clients renew.

Most media plans are still organized by channel. Search budget here, social budget there, streaming over in a separate deck. AI makes that structure obsolete, because it can plan around the customer instead.

Take a regional home services company running paid search and social. Those campaigns are producing something more valuable than clicks: a live stream of households actively researching its services. AI can turn those signals into a defined first-party audience, figure out where else those households spend their time, and extend the plan into streaming TV, digital video and audio to reach them there. One audience, coordinated across channels, built from the client’s own demand rather than a platform’s inventory.

That is a different kind of planning. The question stops being “what should we spend on CTV” and becomes “who are we trying to reach, and where are they?”

The next step is letting AI act on that intelligence. Agentic systems can take audience and performance signals and adjust targeting, channel mix and spend as conditions change, instead of waiting for the monthly optimization meeting. Static plans with quarterly reviews are a relic of how long it used to take humans to read the data.

But here is where agencies should be careful. The advantage is not how much of the buying you automate. Anyone can automate. The advantage is whether the agent is optimizing toward something that matters. An agent chasing cheaper CPMs will find them, and the client will not care. An agent optimizing toward the client’s actual business outcomes, with the agency keeping full transparency and control, is a different product entirely.

This is the part most of the industry still avoids. Clicks and impressions are easy to report and nearly impossible to defend in a budget conversation. The only metrics a CFO cares about are sales, customers and revenue.

Once that loop exists, everything upstream improves. Planning gets smarter because it is fed by real conversions. Agentic buying gets better because it is optimizing toward revenue instead of proxies. And the agency walks into every renewal with proof rather than a deck.

Agencies are already monetizing AI. Digiday’s report found that 59% are launching new AI-powered products, 34% are selling strategic roadmaps or audits and 31% are charging additional fees for AI-powered services. But if the product is “we have AI,” the pricing power will not last. Every competitor has AI. Within a year, every client will have it too.

The agencies that win the next cycle will not be the ones with the most AI. They will be the ones who can show a client exactly what it delivered, in the client’s own numbers. Efficiency gets agencies a thank-you. Outcomes get agencies the next three years of the account.Partner insights from fullthrottle.ai

While creators are welcoming brands’ no-AI demands, both sides need to agree upfront on whether creators are prohibited from using AI tools in any part of their process, according to Pearpop’s Kurt Stadelman.

#7216. The next test for agentic media

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Anudit Vikram, Chief Product Officer, Channel Factory

Advertisers are building AI systems around the way their teams already work. These teams have their own agents, their own data and their own rules for what an agent may and may not do. At the same time, most of the specialized intelligence they depend on still sits inside separate vendor platforms.

As a result, vendor intelligence shapes a recommendation while remaining disconnected from the system acting on the recommendation. When planners carry conclusions between tools, the evidence and conditions behind those recommendations often fall away.

That’s the problem agentic media has to solve next. Can the intelligence behind the agent go where the work happens?

Models and agent frameworks are widely available. Two vendors can build on the same foundation and ship agents that look identical in a demo. What separates them is what the agent knows, and whether the advertiser can put that knowledge to work. In media buying, that knowledge is specific.

A broad content classification offers only a starting point for a media decision. Suitability depends on the particular advertiser and market, and those judgments can change quickly when breaking news alters the significance of a placement. A general-purpose model can’t reconstruct that brand-specific knowledge or historical context from a prompt alone. Classification depth and performance history can.

For Channel Factory, that means IAB 3.0 classification across more than 15 million YouTube channels, and more than 10 years and $4 billion of campaign performance data. To be clear, this isn’t an argument for keeping that intelligence locked inside a platform; it’s the opposite. Intelligence that only works inside its owner’s interface is as limited as a capable agent no one can reach.

So the test is portability. Can an advertiser’s own agent call on a provider’s intelligence directly, from inside the planning and buying environment it already uses? That’s how Channel Factory is building, with contextual and suitability intelligence made available through APIs and MCP servers, and delivered through modular agents that run inside an advertiser’s environment or alongside it.

With this architecture, market-specific suitability analysis becomes part of an advertiser’s own planning process. An agent can evaluate inventory against the provider’s classifications and historical performance data, applying the relevant standards for each market. Those findings can directly inform the plan, with the advertiser’s existing rules governing what the agent can recommend or change.

Portable intelligence doesn’t mean identical agents. Two advertisers can draw on the same underlying intelligence and make different decisions. For instance, one avoids content when signals are unclear while another favors reaching inside defined boundaries. Both are valid because the thresholds, priorities and approval rules are the advertisers’.

An agent’s knowledge source shouldn’t determine the scope of its authority. The brand decides what acceptable judgment looks like. The provider keeps the classification, history and models current.

Learning needs the same discipline. Feedback and outcomes should refine recommendations, but advertisers should decide what shapes future decisions and which policies stay fixed. On its own, a successful campaign shouldn’t rewrite a brand’s suitability standards.

Intelligence that moves into someone else’s systems has to carry its accountability along. As agents shift from recommending to acting, teams need to see what changed, why and whether it stayed within approved limits.

The principle is simple: Agents do the reasoning, and the engine owns the state, so every action is authorized, logged and traceable to the signals behind it. People define the strategy. Autonomy is earned in stages: alerts first, then recommendations, then bounded actions. An agent might make a small budget adjustment under set conditions while sending a larger change for human approval. How much authority to delegate is the advertiser’s call, and the system should make that call easy to enforce and easy to review.

As agentic capabilities spread, feature lists will converge. Advertisers should look underneath the interface and put three questions to any provider:

The providers who can answer those clearly will matter more as brands take greater control of how they buy media. The rest will be unrealized demos.

#7213. Amazon is buying ChatGPT ads while blocking OpenAI crawlers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Amazon is paying OpenAI to reach the same shoppers it refuses to let ChatGPT study.

After a relatively quiet few months on the chatbot following its Prime Day activity, the e-commerce giant has returned to the platform with a new round of Black Friday ads in the U.S., Digiday has seen.

The ads, which are the same format as typical ChatGPT ads — separate from the user conversation, with a favicon, headline, description and a “Learn more” call to action — are appearing in ChatGPT in the lead-up to Black Friday. They promote Amazon Black Friday deals and direct users to Amazon to complete their purchases.

What’s different compared to other retailers, though, is how ChatGPT is surfacing Amazon products and deals. Rather than directing users to individual product pages, ChatGPT’s shopping experience is linking to Amazon search results, while drawing on third-party deal-tracking websites to identify products, discounts and up-to-date pricing. That means ChatGPT can help consumers discover Amazon products and compare deals without necessarily accessing Amazon’s own website directly.

Amazon’s increased activity on ChatGPT is interesting given the retailer already operates its own shopping ecosystem and one of the world’s largest ad businesses. Its presence on the chatbot puts it in the position of using a competing platform to drive shoppers back to its own marketplace, while that same platform is developing its own way of facilitating product discovery around Amazon’s catalogue.

Amazon also has an opportunity to monetize that traffic once shoppers arrive on its marketplace.

“Sending shoppers to Amazon search results rather than individual product pages also creates another opportunity to make money from advertising,” said Arielle Feger, senior analyst, commerce media and retail at eMarketer.

Amazon’s U.S. search ad revenues are forecasted to reach $40.57 billion this year, nearly a quarter of all U.S. search ad spending. Traffic coming from ChatGPT gives Amazon another opportunity to put sponsored products in front of shoppers.

“For brands, that creates a risk of paying twice for the same customer,” Feger added. “Even after winning a recommendation in ChatGPT, a brand could find itself paying again to compete against sponsored listings on Amazon.”

But Amazon’s relationship with ChatGPT extends beyond buying ad space for its own products.

In September, Amazon Ads announced a partnership with OpenAI that allows advertisers to buy ChatGPT inventory through Amazon’s DSP, extending their existing Amazon ad campaigns onto the chatbot. The integration initially gives select U.S. advertisers access to ChatGPT’s ad inventory through Amazon DSP, giving brands another route to reach consumers using the AI chatbot.

What is unclear is whether Amazon has bought its ChatGPT ads directly from OpenAI, or through its own DSP.

Both Amazon and OpenAI declined to comment on this article.

#7207. Ad Tech Briefing: Meta eyes CTV as AppLovin circles Criteo, signs of the squeeze on independent ad tech

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

AppLovin’s reported interest in Criteo and Meta’s plans to explore CTV could put further pressure on independent ad tech, with some companies reporting challenging circumstances in the latest round of earnings calls.

Last week, Reuters reported unconfirmed acquisition discussions involving AppLovin and Criteo, the same day Next in Media reported that Meta was exploring partnerships with television manufacturers and free FAST services to extend its advertising platform into CTV.

If both sets of developments materialize, it would represent two of the largest established (mobile) ad platforms expanding into new channels using existing advertiser relationships, data, and buying technology.

It’s well understood that AppLovin wants to extend its performance advertising business beyond mobile applications, and Criteo — a company that is reportedly seeking an exit from the public markets — could provide retail relationships, commerce data, and access to advertisers across the open web.

Meanwhile, Meta could use its existing buying platform to help advertisers reach television audiences without relying on a separate demand-side platform.

In recent weeks, separate sources told Digiday this could prove a key advantage in helping the social networking outfit eat into Google’s lead in markets where its DSP, DV 360, dominates.

However, developments could reduce advertisers’ dependence on independent DSPs and supply-side platforms.

AppLovin’s potential shortcut into commerce

Private equity firms Vista Equity Partners and Quinti Capital have also reportedly expressed interest in Criteo, which has engaged an investment bank to evaluate its options.

AppLovin reported third-quarter revenue of $2.19 billion, up 55% year over year, alongside $1.44 billion in net income.

Criteo reported a 12% revenue decline to $416 million, with net income falling to approximately $6 million.

AppLovin’s growth has been driven by mobile advertising, although the company is increasingly targeting e-commerce advertisers outside of gaming.

Criteo has spent years expanding beyond retargeting into commerce media, developing relationships with retailers, access to shopper data and cross-channel advertising capabilities.

Per official figures, Criteo’s commerce intelligence draws on more than $1 trillion in annual transactions, 740 million daily active users and over 5 billion product SKUs, and acquiring it could accelerate AppLovin’s expansion into retail media, providing relationships and data it would otherwise need to develop independently.

However, integrating the businesses would be complicated given that AppLovin’s core technology is built around mobile apps, while Criteo operates across retailers, publishers, and other commerce environments.

The potential benefits would depend on whether their different data assets, buying systems, and commercial relationships could work effectively when integrated.

Earlier in the year, multiple sources told Digiday that Meta is ⁠exploring an option that would allow advertisers to extend campaigns into third-party television inventory through its existing advertising tools — a measurement tactic commonly known as audience extension.

The latest reporting suggests Meta is considering how those relationships could support an advertiser-facing buying option, resembling Pinterest’s CTV audience-extension offering, which lets advertisers use Pinterest’s audience data to reach consumers beyond its owned properties. Meta could pursue a similar approach at considerably greater scale.

Its existing advertiser base could access television inventory without establishing separate relationships with specialist CTV buying platforms, an attractive option for smaller advertisers already using Meta’s automated campaign tools.

For DSPs and SSPs, the competitive implications would be significant, as Meta could bring more advertisers directly into CTV, potentially reducing the role of intermediaries that connect buyers with television inventory.

But questions remain about how the proposed model would operate. For example, would Meta determine which inventory advertisers purchase? Plus, how would fees and media costs be disclosed? And what independent measurement would be available?

Such details are likely to determine whether Meta’s proposed offering creates a meaningful alternative to existing CTV buying arrangements or simply adds another layer between advertisers and publishers.

AppLovin’s interest in Criteo and Meta’s reported CTV expansion reflect a broader shift in advertising technology, as larger companies with bigger advertiser bases and proprietary data extend their buying capabilities into additional channels. Amazon is pursuing a similar strategy through its DSP and retail media business, which is morphing into a unified agentic platform.

The Trade Desk’s recent financial performance illustrates the difficulties facing some independent players. The company reported a 0.5% third-quarter revenue decline to $736 million, marking a rare contraction after years of sustained growth, and is on course to simplify and offer new measurement capabilities as it attempts to restore growth.

While The Trade Desk’s recent revenue decline cannot be attributed to Meta’s reported plans or AppLovin’s potential acquisition, the contrast provides context for broader competitive pressure on independent buying platforms.

The emergence of unified media platforms is another response, as ⁠last week’s explainer outlined; these systems aim to coordinate advertising across search, social, programmatic, and direct publisher relationships.

But coordinating buying across platforms is not the same as controlling the underlying inventory, data, or optimization technology. Independent companies will need to demonstrate value beyond simplifying workflows, particularly as AI makes campaign execution easier.

For independent ad tech, maintaining a meaningful role in that environment will become increasingly difficult.

Figures from ⁠HUMAN Security’s BADBOX 3.0 investigation, published Nov. 18.

The industry’s emerging buying platforms promise to coordinate advertising across channels, but their ability to control actual spending decisions remains contested.

⁠Ad Tech Briefing: Google’s antitrust troubles aren’t over

Google’s European antitrust exposure and The Trade Desk’s financial difficulties illustrate the challenges facing established ad tech businesses.

⁠Anthropic says no to ads, but Wall Street may have other ideas

Anthropic’s opposition to advertising raises questions about whether the company can maintain that position as the cost of developing and operating AI models increases.

⁠Ampersand is offloading its indie agency business to Comcast and Spectrum following mass layoffs

Ampersand’s restructuring and the transfer of its independent agency business to Comcast and Spectrum highlight the pressures facing traditional television advertising intermediaries.

⁠AI-driven tech layoffs suck, but might lead to certification

Jonathan Bellack examines how AI coding tools are reducing opportunities for junior engineers, and considers whether professional certification could help establish new standards for software development.

HUMAN Security’s latest investigation queries the effectiveness of CTV verification safeguards.

The shift has been playing for the best part of a decade. COMvergence’s first Global Advertiser Survey shows had far its got. T

#7202. Walmart is letting Meta’s Muse shop its shelves — but on its own terms

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

By letting Meta’s Muse agent into its stores only through the gateway it built and controls, Walmart is treating AI agent access the way retail media networks once treated ad slots: as inventory to be doled out on its own terms.

The retailer is preparing to launch a connector to Muse that will provide the AI agent with defined access to its e-commerce infrastructure, meaning it gets to set the guardrails and boundaries within which Meta’s agent can browse and shop.

The capability was noticed by Juozas Kaziukėnas, founder of e-commerce research firm Marketplace Pulse, after he spotted an update to Walmart’s technical instruction manual for Muse. According to The Verge, that integration launches tomorrow (Nov. 24), in time for Black Friday Cyber Monday shopping.

The manual, which Digiday has also seen and verified, describes how Muse can interact with Walmart’s shopping systems on behalf of a user. This includes tasks like searching for products, answering questions, building shopping carts and, if permission is granted, completing orders. Muse can also call on Walmart’s own shopping assistant Sparky to answer questions about products, stores and comparisons.

So a Walmart Shopper could ask Muse to compare three televisions, find specific ingredients for a dinner party, or ask the agent to add products to its Walmart shopping cart. Muse can then use Walmart’s systems to carry out those tasks. For example, Walmart could help Muse identify a customer’s usual brand of milk without handing over their entire shopping history.

This goes beyond what someone might typically expect from a chatbot, where a user might simply ask for recommendations. Instead, Muse, through the gateway to Walmart’s own marketplace, can act on those recommendations, taking care of the legwork involved in shopping and even making purchases once the user has given the necessary permission.

Walmart talked about its vision for this in its blog post last month.

Built using the Model Context Protocol (MCP), the integration gives AI agents a more direct and reliable way to interact with Walmart’s shopping systems. It’s designed to let agents shop on a customer’s behalf without giving them access to their wider purchase history or unnecessary personal information. For example, Walmart could help Muse identify a customer’s usual brand of milk without handing over their entire shopping history.

Walmart has also started introducing Agent Profiles, which gives each AI agent its own dedicated profile within a customer’s Walmart account. It means shoppers can distinguish purchases made by an AI agent from the ones they’ve completed themselves, while deciding how much autonomy they want to give the agent. Customers can require approval for every purchase, or set a spending limit (such as $50, $100 or $200), allowing the agent to spend up to that amount without seeking approval each time. Customers also have the ability to disable the agent’s access at any time.

This development comes after Amazon already blocked Muse from accessing its e-commerce site in September, arguing that third-party AI agents should operate openly and respect retailers’ decisions about whether to participate. At the time, Amazon said it asked Meta to remove its website from Muse’s shopping experience, but Meta declined. It also raised concerns about Muse accessing customer accounts without authorization and potentially capturing customer credentials. However, Meta disputed the claims, saying Muse cannot see users’ passwords or payment details.

While Walmart might be working closer with Muse going forward, this approach could raise questions about its ad business. In particular, with Muse increasingly allowed to take over product discovery, where does that leave its sponsored product placements and how much influence will brands have over what an AI agent recommends.

“There will emerge a new advertising mechanism where brands will pay dollars not for a sponsored placement, but for a ‘sponsored consideration’ to the agents,” said Abhishek Bora, head of digital growth at All Star Elite. “If agents take over the buying process, how would a new brand with a better product and offer get considered by an agent when it has no history of reviews, usage or purchases?”

Neither Walmart nor Meta responded to Digiday’s request for comment.

Amazon is paying to reach ChatGPT shoppers while the chatbot is finding other ways to surface its products and deals.

Holiday marketing is changing: CTV/streaming and TV ads are gaining traction in 2026.

Gap Inc. has tapped boy band Just Your Type (JYT) for a multi-year partnership and launched “The Agency,” a scripted microseries made for Banana Republic.

#7200. Why brands are writing no-AI clauses into creator deals

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Influencer marketing contracts have become a focal point in the creator economy. There’s heightened attention being paid by brands, creators and creators’ representatives to deal terms, such as usage rights, exclusivity and payment terms. But among the newest addendums is AI usage.

“We actually are seeing more and more contracts including language around the creator not being able to use AI in their content for these campaigns,” said Kurt Stadelman, vp of talent at creator marketing firm Pearpop, in a conversation with Digiday managing editor Sara Jerde during Digiday’s recent Creator Strategies event.

Creators are actually welcoming brands’ no-AI demands, he said. Creators are already concerned about generative AI being used to create content and crowd out creators from people’s feeds. So brands forbidding AI-generated content from creators signals a commitment by brands to human-made content, said Stadelman.

But what counts as AI-generated content? Does it prohibit the creator from using AI tools in any part of their process?

“We’ve had to specify that a few times just to make sure, because obviously you want the creator to be able to use AI tools in the background when they’re developing their concept or their script. Even utilizing AI to analyze their past performance of their content and advise them on their strategy moving forward. And I think brands should probably embrace that because it ultimately helps the creator deliver the best version of themselves and the best content possible,” said Stadelman.

Here are a few highlights from the conversation, which have been edited for length and clarity.

It’s way too risky to be only utilizing organic posting for creator campaigns and just hoping that the right people see it. We are seeing more and more brands utilize paid behind whether it’s white-listing or utilizing the creator content as an ad unit for themselves on their own channels. Along with that comes, obviously, you have to write the paid usage rights into the contract, and most brands are good about that.

Usage in perpetuity should never be agreed to. That’s a dangerous place to be for a creator and can negatively impact their future collaborations with other brands in the same category. Exclusivity, obviously, is quite difficult for a lot of creators if they agree to a longer exclusivity term than they should. That limits their options moving forward.

Sometimes you’ll see proposed terms that are just a little too lengthy with the usage rights. We push back on that sometimes. I don’t think there’s any reason to be agreeing upfront to any more than six months of paid usage. Even that’s a bit lengthy. Starting it at three months if they’re really wanting to utilize paid with the campaign, that makes sense. Even up to six months is fine in most cases. But once you get to that point six months from now, you can reassess and extend if you’d like. But I don’t think there’s any reason to go beyond that in the initial contract.

We always push for shorter payment terms. If somebody’s asking for net-90, we try to push for net-60 or, ideally, net-30. But I understand that some brands have their standard operating procedures there, which is fine. But we just ask that they honor those payment terms, which is a whole other issue that we’re seeing happen more and more, is that brands are just not honoring these payment terms that they’ve themselves written into the contract.

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at the latest call-to-action for the CTV advertising industry to tackle its transparency problem.

In a live Digiday Podcast recording, YSL Beauty’s Hadley Musinsky and Night’s Sophie Bressler broke down how deals between brands and influencers come together.

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at creator Grace Wells’s full production process, from initial brief to final delivery.

#7196. Product Manager - Postgres at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're hiring a Product Manager to own Postgres itself at Supabase. Every Supabase project is a full Postgres database, and Postgres is the foundation that Auth, Storage, Realtime and Edge Functions run on. You'll own the product experience of the database: Postgres versions and upgrades, maintenance, observability, extensions and security.

You'll work closely with our Postgres engineering teams and with the PM lead for Databases, who leads Multigres. You'll define the Postgres requirements that architecture must meet, and own how customers experience their database through that transition.

This role is ideal for someone who loves Postgres, sweats the details that make a database trustworthy, and is excited about improving the experience for millions of developers. Small improvements here compound across every project on the platform.

You'll own Postgres versions, upgrades and end-of-life. Which major and minor versions we support, how and when customers upgrade, and how we retire old versions. Your goal is to make upgrades routine rather than risky.

You'll make maintenance predictable. You'll design how we schedule, communicate and run maintenance windows so customers stay informed and in control, and disruption is rare and expected.

You'll help developers understand their database. Query performance, metrics, logs and advisors: you'll own the path from "my database is slow" to the fix.

You'll own our extension ecosystem. Which extensions we offer, how we evaluate and support new ones, and how we keep them working across versions.

You'll own Postgres security. Roles and permissions, Row Level Security ergonomics, secure defaults, and how we respond to Postgres and extension CVEs.

You'll talk to developers across the full spectrum, from indie developers on their first project to enterprises running critical workloads, and bring what you learn into every prioritization call.

You'll own the problem definition, requirements and success metrics for what we ship, such as upgrade success rate, the share of the fleet on supported versions, and time-to-diagnose for performance problems. You'll set the metric before the team builds, then track it after launch.

You'll keep Engineering, Support and GTM aligned. You'll own the plan of record for what's coming, why, and in what order, and communicate version changes and deprecations clearly to customers.

4+ years of Product Management experience on databases, developer tools or infrastructure, or equivalent depth as an engineer, DBA or founder moving into product. You're comfortable commenting on an architecture doc and advocating for the user when trade-offs are required.

Know Postgres well, or another relational database well enough to ramp quickly. You're comfortable reading EXPLAIN output and talking about replication, WAL, roles and extensions.

Have shipped products where reliability and trust are the experience. You treat downtime and breaking changes as product problems, not just operational ones.

Write clearly. Your specs, announcements and deprecation notices leave little room for confusion.

Build with what you own. You run queries, test upgrades and try extensions yourself, and form opinions through first-hand use.

Use AI to move faster. You use AI tools to compress research, drafting and feedback synthesis so you spend more time on judgment calls.

Work async by default. Supabase is fully remote. You make decisions in writing and move work forward without meetings.

Ran Postgres in production: upgrades, tuning, incident response.

Worked on database observability, performance tooling, or security and compliance products.

Contributed to Postgres, its extensions, or the wider open-source community.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7189. Product Builder at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Curious about the kind of challenges you'll work on at Camunda? Watch this quick 30-minute talk from our engineers to learn more about the new Camunda Exporter and how we’re solving complex problems at scale

We are looking for a Product Builder to join the IDP & RPA pod. You will take real problems from customers, decide what is worth solving, and build and ship the solution yourself, using AI tools to get from idea to production quickly without lowering the bar.

Product Building is how Camunda's Experience organization builds the applications people use on top of Camunda 8, such as Hub, Operate, and ProcessOS. Small pods of builders own a product area from customer problem to shipped change, with no hand-offs between product management, design, and engineering. We are setting this model up now, so you will help shape how it works.

The IDP & RPA pod builds how Camunda processes work with documents and legacy applications. With intelligent document processing (IDP), customers create extraction and classification templates in Camunda Hub and publish them so a process can use AI to turn invoices and other documents into structured data. With robotic process automation (RPA), customers integrate legacy applications that have no APIs, using Robot Framework scripts on a standalone RPA worker. The hard problems come with putting LLMs into customer processes: extraction quality, model and prompt choices, testing and trust, and working for AI agents as well as for people.

Help shape product direction for IDP & RPA by combining customer input, usage data, and field feedback into clear priorities.

Partner with the other builders and product strategy to turn problems into shipped features, using short written proposals and prototypes to align early.

Make judgment calls with incomplete information within your scope and own the outcome.

Communicate trade-offs and decisions clearly to stakeholders who don't live in the details the way you do.

Use AI tools across the whole loop, from specs to prototypes to production-ready code, and hold a high bar for what you ship by reviewing and improving the output rather than accepting it.

Build, test, and ship improvements to the IDP & RPA experience, reading errors and tracing issues far enough to give your pod useful context.

Talk to customers and to support and customer success colleagues about how IDP is used and where it breaks, and turn what you learn into decisions about what to build next.

Pick up customer incidents and trace them across Hub, the connectors, and the deployed process of the customer.

Track what you ship against real outcomes and adjust when the data tells you to.

#7175. Information Security Engineer - Application Security at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Hi there! We're hiring an Information Security Engineer with a focus on Application Security to join our Information Security team at Camunda.

We are a team that takes security seriously without making it feel like a locked door. We work in a transparent, straightforward way, and we care about doing the work right rather than just checking a compliance box.

You will work side by side with our R&D engineers: reviewing designs, threat modeling new features, digging into code and helping teams make good, risk-based decisions about the vulnerabilities that come in. You'll also help build the internal tools that make this work scale, including our CVE registry and the LLM-assisted workflows we use to analyse code and triage findings.

Camunda is an AI-first company, and InfoSec is no exception: AI agents are part of how we review code, assess vulnerabilities, and build our tools. You will help shape how that works.

This is a hands-on role for someone who already has solid AppSec foundations and wants to grow. We are a generalist team, so you will also take part in our on-call rotation and incident response, and get exposure to other areas of security. But most of your time will be spent with engineers and building and maintaining internal tools and processes.

Vulnerability management: triage findings from SCA, container and code scanning, external reports and internal security testing; assess whether Camunda is actually affected; agree fix / mitigate / accept with engineering and follow through to release.

Threat modeling and security reviews: run threat modeling sessions and design reviews with R&D teams for new features and architecture changes, and turn the results into concrete, prioritised actions.

Code analysis with LLMs: use and improve LLM-assisted tooling (agents, prompts, reusable skills) to review code, validate findings and reproduce issues, while applying your own judgement to know when the model is wrong.

Build internal security tooling: contribute to the CVE registry and related automation that connects scanners, engineering teams, and customer communication. Much of it is built on Camunda itself.

Help engineers do the secure thing by default: answer questions, review pull requests, improve guidelines and occasionally run a session or workshop.

On-call and incident response: share the InfoSec on-call rotation with the other engineers and help investigate and resolve security incidents.

Support the wider team: help write security advisories for our customers, answer customer security questions and support audits.

Around 3+ years in security or software engineering, with at least 2 of them working on application or product security (AppSec, product security, pentesting, or a security-focused developer role all count).

Experience at a SaaS or software product company, working closely with engineering teams that ship often through modern CI/CD and cloud environments.

Hands-on vulnerability triage: you've worked with tools like Snyk, Trivy, Semgrep, CodeQL or similar, can tell a real issue from a false positive, and can explain how exploitable something actually is in context (CVSS, exploitability, configuration).

Threat modeling and design review experience (e.g. STRIDE) on real systems, ideally web apps, APIs, or distributed services.

You read and write code. You're comfortable reading Java (Camunda is built on it) well enough to follow a data flow and spot a security bug. You can write scripts and small tools and contribute to an existing codebase with review.

You build with AI, not just chat with it. You use AI coding agents (Claude Code, Cursor, Copilot or similar) in your daily work to write tools, analyse code or automate tasks. And you know their output still needs a second look, especially when it comes to security.

Clear, friendly communication with engineers. You see yourself as an enabler, not a gatekeeper, and you can explain risk to people who aren't security specialists.

Some Python and/or JavaScript experience, or experience building internal tools.

Willingness to learn and use Camunda, since we build a lot of our own security automation on it. Prior BPMN / workflow experience is a plus.

Experience responding to security incidents or being part of an on-call rotation.

Exposure to security advisories, CVE disclosure or bug bounty programmes.

Knowledge of AI/LLM security risks (OWASP Top 10 for LLMs / agentic apps, prompt injection, tool abuse).

Experience securing Kubernetes or container-based workloads, or open-source / B2B software.

#7173. 5 Questions For…CNBC’s Eamon Javers on His Call With President Trump on Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Reinstatement

What should have been a slow news week with the Thanksgiving holiday approaching was turned on its head Monday morning, when news broke that Nvidia had reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO.

This comes after Huang recently stepped down following revelations of multiple extramarital relationships, including with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy.

CNBC was one of the first networks to break the news at 8:56 a.m. ET, and was also the first to speak with President Donald Trump following the reinstatement.

This is the moment the Jensen Huang news broke on @CNBC: pic.twitter.com/YKrRxLt7Ul

— Brian Stelter (@brianstelter) November 23, 2026

Eamon Javers, CNBC’s senior Washington correspondent, was the one who initially talked to the president, telling TVNewser that Trump called him as he was headed to CNBC’s DC bureau.

In a follow-up to our recent 5 Questions For… series, which covered Huang’s unexpected departure, Javers details how he learned of the reinstatement, his conversation with the president, and the coordination involved with the control room.

How did you hear about the news of Jensen Huang’s return?

I was walking my daughter to her bus stop this morning, and my phone blew up with texts from our bureau. It’s maybe the most important business story of the year, and I was a little frustrated that I was out of position when it broke. So I began texting sources for comment.

TVNewser: How did the call with President Trump occur?

Javers: He called me as I was in my car on the way to our CNBC bureau. I wasn’t expecting it at all. But we have talked on the phone from time to time, and I had tried to reach him earlier in the morning for his comment. So, I pulled over on the side of the road, called my desk, and was able to patch into the control room for a live interview. The whole process took about 4 minutes, and then we went live for nearly an hour. Our producers, control room team, and anchors did an incredible job of rolling with it and reacting very, very quickly so we could make it happen.

TVN: What was it like to talk to the president in real time during such a unique moment?

Javers: It’s always an honor to talk to the president of the United States, and today it was a unique opportunity because there was so much that was unknown about the role he played behind the scenes. I was focused on all of the aspects that were still unknown, and trying to get the president to explain the who, what, when, and why of how this happened.

TVN: How were you coordinating the conversation with the newsroom and control room?

Javers: We didn’t coordinate it or prep in advance at all. It happened too quickly. And your audience will understand the technical aspect of this, which is that on a phoner, you can’t communicate with the control room during the interview. On TV, they can speak to you in your earpiece to communicate how long you have left, if the anchors want to get in a question, and all of the production stuff you need in real time. Here it was just — do it live.

TV: What are the business ramifications of such a re-appointment?

Javers: We’ve really never seen anything this extraordinary in corporate America — at least in my lifetime. I think it’s too early to say how this will play out, and certainly too early to say whether it will be successful or not. One thing is for sure: This story isn’t over yet.

Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

This was the second week in a row World News Tonight had over 9 million total viewers

#7163. Attorney, New Verticals at Atticus

At any given time, 16 million Americans are experiencing a crisis that requires urgent help from our legal system or government. The right assistance could transform their lives. But today, most never get it.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured person in crisis to get the life-changing aid they deserve. In the last six years, we've become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We’re also quickly scaling an injury practice to help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we've helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10B in life-changing aid — and earned over 20,000+ five-star reviews. And we're just getting started.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, GV (Google Ventures), and True Ventures, with ambitions to create a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

Each month, tens of thousands of Americans seek our help for a single legal matter – applying for Social Security Disability, filing a Workers’ Compensation claim – but many of these clients are actually eligible for additional aid without ever knowing it.

Your job as Lead Attorney, New Verticals is to help us do right by these clients, helping them claim additional benefits through the social safety net. You’ll oversee the team of intake specialists who speak with clients who have secondary claims, evaluating the case facts and whether to connect them with an Atticus attorney or local attorney to provide legal advice and potentially take on their case.

This is a job that straddles the line between practicing the law and growing a business. It requires developing a strong understanding of multiple fields of law, and being able to accurately identify when we can help and when we cannot. But even more importantly, this job requires you to be an incredibly strong operator: you need to keep the big picture in mind and stay laser-focused on how we can achieve our goals, oftentimes finding outside-the-box ways to accomplish them. You will report to our General Manager of New Verticals and work closely with our CEO and Managing Attorney.

On any given day, you’ll spend your time:

Speaking with severely injured clients trying to rebuild their lives after a catastrophic accident

Sourcing and vetting local counsel in individual markets, holding a high bar for representation quality and ending relationships that fall below it

Working with our data and insights team to identify existing clients who may have unrecognized claims in adjacent fields of law, and setting the standard for which ancillary cases we take on

Building the playbook for claim types we haven't handled before, starting by screening cases yourself and eventually creating the protocol our intake specialists will run at scale

This is a rare opportunity for an attorney to join a profitable and growing public-interest law firm and take on one of our biggest priorities as a company.

You can understand the nuances of various contingency fields of law and can apply them to a variety of cases (including Social Security Disability, Workers’ Compensation, Personal Injury, and more) – you don’t need to be an expert today, but you should be a fast learner.

You can empathize with anyone and quickly build rapport and relationships while exercising good judgment, both with our clients and local counsel at law firms across the country.

You have strong instincts and judgment as an operator – you can run effective and efficient processes, and you don’t let them run you.

You have a demonstrated commitment to doing good and helping people.

You’ve built a track record as a great people manager – you know how to motivate talent and hold a high bar for output.

You have meaningful work experience outside of serving as a lawyer, ideally at a startup or other fast-paced, agile environment (e.g., a business operations team).

We are strongly committed to building a diverse team. If you're from a background that's underrepresented in tech, we'd love to meet you.

This role offers a unique chance to join a startup that has strong traction (substantial funding, well-respected backers, tremendous growth, and many happy customers) but is still small enough that you can have a huge impact and play a role in shaping our success.

We're a certified B Corporation tackling a critical social problem. Our mission to help people in need drives everything we do, and your work here will touch many lives.

We offer competitive pay — including equity — and generous benefits:

Medical and dental insurance with 100% of employees and their families premiums covered

15 vacation days & ~20 paid holidays each year (including two weeks at end-of-year)

$1,000 reimbursable stipend for education and training outside of work

Up to $1,200/year student loan repayment assistance

Humble, thoughtful, smart, fun colleagues

We anticipate the base salary band for this role will be between $140,000 and $175,000 in addition to equity and benefits. The salary at offer will be determined by a number of factors such as candidate's experience, knowledge, skills and abilities, as well as internal equity among our team.

This job is fully remote and we’re committed to empowering everyone with flexibility. Work remotely, and travel to LA (on the company dime) as needed to be with your colleagues – usually quarterly, plus offsites. We care a lot about building a great culture and we think some interactions need to happen in person, so we put a lot of thought into retreats, offsites, and other ways to gather.
